# Decisions

What has been decided, and what is proposed and waiting for a yes or a correction. The
other pages are written as if every proposal here stands. When one changes, this page
changes first, then the page it affects, then the code.

A new decision takes the next number and is never renumbered. A reversed decision is
marked as replaced, not removed.

## Decided

| ID | Decision |
|---|---|
| D-01 | The product is named `hirdir` and installs under `/opt/hirdir`. |
| D-02 | Ubuntu 26.04 LTS. Python with FastAPI. SQLite now, PostgreSQL as an option later. nginx from Ubuntu terminates TLS. |
| D-03 | No cookies. The session token is kept in local browser storage. |
| D-04 | The MVP signs in with a username and an access code that an Admin sets. Length and complexity rules come after the MVP. RADIUS comes next, SAML after that. |
| D-05 | The session token is signed by the server and carries its time of issue. Each user has a start time; a token issued before it is refused. |
| D-06 | Roles are View-only, Inventory and Admin. A user group is a label by default. A role may be granted to a user or a group, and the highest wins. |
| D-07 | Automation tokens belong to service accounts, not to people. |
| D-08 | Nothing is served without signing in except the sign-in page and the health check. |
| D-09 | No compliance regime is mandated. Audit records are kept without expiry until a retention feature exists. Security controls that `hirdir` does not implement are referred to, not claimed. |
| D-10 | Python is built with uv, ty and ruff. The `.deb` carries Python and every Python dependency. Ordinary Ubuntu packages are left to apt. |
| D-11 | Every release uses the latest production release of every component. A fault not readily overcome earns a one-release hold, stated in the release notes. |
| D-12 | Release notes: a summary of one to three sentences; New, Changed, Fixes and Additional Notes as needed; a lines-of-code table from `cloc`; a versions table. |
| D-13 | Every Markdown file is published as HTML on GitHub Pages, with pictures from `images/`. |
| D-14 | The package installs nginx snippets and expects an existing site to supply TLS. A setup script edits the nginx configuration for early deployments. |
| D-15 | Tested on the build host in rootful Docker or a systemd container. A container image is a later packaging option. |
| D-16 | The inventory holds what has a recurring support contract or adds support burden, including virtual machines, software and services. |
| D-17 | Every record has an application-assigned ID, the HID. |
| D-18 | Manufacturer, PID and serial number identify an item. Serial number first, asset tag when present, name when neither exists. A blank name takes the hostname. |
| D-19 | MAC address is a first-class, searchable field and is not unique. |
| D-20 | Items are major or minor. Major items count for lifecycle and support weight. Minor items do not. |
| D-21 | A stack has one master and one or more members. Each member is counted, covered and costed on its own. The stack is one device on the network. Its serial number is read from the master and not stored. **What it says of the master is changed by D-93:** the master is optional, and the one that is seen is kept apart from the one intended. |
| D-22 | The stack discount on support weight is a global value that a model may override. It never applies to contract cost. |
| D-23 | Device groups nest and carry roles, and cover stacks, clusters and controllers with their endpoints. Registration with a cloud service or SDN controller is a link to an external-system record, not a group. |
| D-24 | The catalog holds, per manufacturer and PID: type, function, description, four end-of-life dates, estimated value, support weight and annual support cost. An item inherits them and may override. |
| D-25 | Estimated value is a planning estimate, optional. Annual support cost works the same way and is expected to be empty at first. |
| D-26 | Spare is a disposition, as Production is. Online or offline is intent, held separately from what monitoring observes. Excess is offline. |
| D-27 | A purchase request records model and quantity against an RITM and is held pending until delivery is assured. |
| D-28 | Addresses may carry a network label (a VRF, a tenant, a colour). |
| D-29 | Tags apply to most records. |
| D-30 | Phones and access points are in the inventory and in the count. |
| D-31 | The grid selects rows only in early versions, with sorting by any column. AG Grid Community is the grid. |
| D-32 | The repository is public. Examples are fictional. Nothing from a real network is committed. |
| D-33 | The nine lifecycle states stay as drawn. Disposition and expected-online are two further fields, valid in the combinations tabled in [data-model.md](data-model.md). A spare on a shelf at its site is Prod, Spare, offline. A spare still parked with the team is Assigned, Spare. (Was P-01.) |
| D-34 | Component count takes states Assigned through Pending-Removal. Support weight takes Test, Prod and Down, so an offline spare in Prod carries weight and a parked one does not. Both lists are settings. (Was P-02.) |
| D-35 | The headline component count takes hardware and virtual machines. Software and services are reported beside it, and all four kinds carry weight and cost. (Was P-03.) |
| D-36 | The web UI is React with Vite. (Was P-04.) |
| D-37 | Each endpoint carries only its own support weight. A controller gets nothing extra for the endpoints it manages. (Was O-02.) |
| D-38 | For software and services, quantity multiplies both weight and cost. (Was O-03.) |
| D-39 | Support weight is demand, balanced against resource units supplied by staff positions, professional services, smart hands, tools and add-on services including agentic AI. The sum of those lines is the capacity available, for the infrastructure and for other work. The purpose is right-sizing the team. |
| D-40 | Work that is not infrastructure, such as projects, a merger or acquisition, or an organizational transition, is entered as demand lines. The balance is supply against infrastructure plus other work. (Was P-20.) |
| D-41 | A staff line is a kind of position and a count. No capacity is attributed to a named person at this phase. View-only may read capacity lines; Inventory may edit them. (Was P-22.) |
| D-42 | Units per position are fixed numbers. Seniority is a separate staff line per level, each with its own units, with one mid-level position as 1.0 by convention. (Was P-23.) |
| D-43 | A tool or service that makes people more effective, AI among them, is a multiplier on the staff lines it amplifies, not a flat number of units standing in for staff. Until multiplier lines are built, after the MVP, such a tool is entered as a supply line with its estimated gain. The formula is in [data-model.md](data-model.md). A tool that takes work to run is an inventory item whose support weight is already demand. (Was P-24.) |
| D-44 | One resource unit, a mid-level position, carries a support weight of 300. This is a placeholder and a setting. (Was O-05.) |
| D-45 | The web UI is built with TypeScript 7. Its compiler has no programming interface, so the usual OpenAPI type generator and typescript-eslint cannot be used with it: types are generated by the project's own `web/scripts/gen-types.mjs`, and the linter is Biome. Holding TypeScript at an older version was considered and declined. |
| D-46 | A time is shown to a person in local time, with the name of its zone: the reader's own zone where it is known, otherwise the installation's `time_zone` setting, which is America/New_York unless changed. A time is not shown in UTC. Storage and the API keep UTC. **Replaced by D-61.** |
| D-47 | The backup taken before an upgrade is one of the rotating backups, and is removed on the same schedule as the others. |
| D-48 | Using `hirdir` should be easy and without waiting. Where a delay can be avoided or hidden, real or perceived, it is: the web UI loads only what a page needs, fetches the rest ahead of need, and a browser keeps what does not change. |
| D-49 | The owner record is in from 0.2.0, with its label, organization and external flag, so that ownership can be shown and tried early with mock-up examples. Its people, and the exception recorded when an item is given to an owner outside the team, come in 0.5.0. (Was P-25.) |
| D-50 | Lifecycle state, disposition and expected-online may be blank on an item. An item with no state is counted, because it may be in the team's hands and nobody has said otherwise. Once a state is given, the state decides: an item declared to be outside the team's control is not counted within it. A count is of the items in the view being looked at, whichever of drafts, active and retired records that view holds. (Was P-26, changed.) |
| D-51 | On a move an item keeps its disposition when the new state allows it, and takes the state's "set on entry" value when it does not. The caller may name the disposition and the intent instead. To be looked at again when a move may set several fields at once. (Was P-27.) |
| D-52 | `session_hours` stays in the configuration file. It moves to the database only if most of the configuration does. (Was P-29.) |
| D-53 | Every text comparison in a filter ignores case, regular expressions included: text is compared as if it were all in lower case. A regular expression matches anywhere in the value unless it is anchored. `ne` also finds blanks. (Was P-30.) |
| D-54 | A list leaves out drafts, retired records and items in Archived unless `include_drafts` or `include_archived` asks for them. The `fields` parameter of a list arrives with export, and `Idempotency-Key` with import, both in 0.3.0. (Was P-31.) |
| D-55 | A decimal has two places: money, support weight and percentages alike. It travels as text in JSON. (Was P-33, changed: support weight had three.) |
| D-56 | `lifecycle_rules` has one row for each state: the dispositions it allows, which of them may and must be online, what is set on entry, and the states an item may move to. The nine states cannot be added to or removed, and no row may make Excess online. (Was P-34.) |
| D-57 | An item that is first entered in Down or Pending-Removal, and so has no disposition "from before", may be given any. Once it has one, that is the one it keeps. (Was P-35.) |
| D-58 | `make package-test` upgrades from the previous release's package: `PREVIOUS_DEB` when set, otherwise the highest version below the one under test in `dist/`. When there is no such package the upgrade is skipped, and the run says so. (Was P-37, changed: it had failed.) |
| D-59 | The migrations of one upgrade run in one transaction. After a failed upgrade the way back is the backup, on the version installed before, and the package prints the commands. (Was P-38.) |
| D-60 | The Admin area of the web UI is shown to every role. Tags, choices and lifecycle rules are in it for all to read, and for Inventory to edit the first two. Users, groups, roles and settings show to an Admin only. (Was P-39.) |
| D-61 | Every time is shown in the application's time zone, with the zone named, so that everybody refers to the same time whatever zone they are in. It is the `time_zone` setting, America/New_York by default. This holds for the web UI, the command line and the names of backup files. Storage and the API keep UTC. (Was P-40, changed. Replaces D-46.) |
| D-62 | A lifecycle move or combination that the rules refuse is a `422` that names the field. `409` stays for a stale version and for uniqueness. (Was P-28.) |
| D-63 | The server's tests use `httpx2`, the continuation of `httpx` under Pydantic. The command line moves to it in 0.3.0, when the client commands are built. (Was P-32.) |
| D-64 | A regular expression is refused before it is compiled when the repeat counts written in it, the numbers in `{n}` and `{n,m}`, multiplied together, come to more than 100. This limits what a pattern may say, never how many records it may find. In the short form of a filter, a value that itself looks like `field:word:` is refused as a mistyped operator. (Was P-36.) |
| D-65 | An import does not turn an inherited value into an override. Where a field inherits and the record has no value of its own, a cell that holds the very value it inherits leaves it inheriting. The same holds for a catalog entry and the global stack discount, and for a row that changes an item's model while it still holds the old model's values. (Was P-43.) |
| D-66 | In an import, a different lifecycle state is a move under the rules of moves, with the row's `state_reason` as the why, and is never forced. A row that would retire a record is rejected. (Was P-45.) |
| D-67 | A preview of more than a hundred rows is worked on a copy of the database. An apply is one transaction and holds the one writer's place while it works; a change that waits more than five seconds behind it is a `503` with `Retry-After`, and was not made. (Was P-51.) |
| D-68 | The packaged nginx snippet sets `client_max_body_size 20m`, the application's own limit and no more. Through nginx a body over 20 MB is refused by nginx, with a `413` and the page of nginx, not a problem document. The application's own `413` is seen through nginx for a file of more than 100,000 rows. A site that wants to take larger files has to change nginx to allow them. (Was P-53.) |
| D-69 | A preview of an import or a bulk edit is kept, and is applied once, by anybody who holds the Inventory role, whoever made it. The job says who made it and who applied it. Apply works the file through again and applies only when every row comes out as previewed and every record a row matched is at the version previewed, rows that were `unchanged` included. (Was P-46, changed: only the one who made a preview could apply it.) |
| D-70 | The largest file `hirdir` takes is `max_megabytes` under `[files]` in the configuration file, 20 unless a site sets another. A site that wants larger files raises it there and raises `client_max_body_size` in nginx to match. The limit of 100,000 rows to a file is not changed by it. |
| D-71 | A row with neither serial number nor asset tag is matched by its name among the items that are not retired, drafts included. It was "the one active item". (Was P-52.) **Changed by D-108:** the item that is found may have a serial number of its own, and a row that gives only a host name is matched on the host name. |
| D-72 | `/etc/nginx/snippets/hirdir.conf` is not a conffile from 0.3.0. The package ships its text as `/usr/share/hirdir/nginx/snippet.conf`, and `hirdir-setup --renew`, which postinst runs at install and at each upgrade after the migrations, writes the snippet from it under the prefix the host has, tests it with `nginx -t`, and has nginx reload when an upgrade changed it. A snippet edited by hand in more than its three prefix lines is left alone, and the upgrade says so. (Was P-54.) |
| D-73 | The CSV and JSON files of `examples/` ship in the package, in `/usr/share/doc/hirdir/examples/`. `install.md` says to load them only where a backup can be put back. (Was P-55.) |
| D-74 | `hirdir admin reset` clears the inventory, so that a site can load test data, try things out, clear it and start over with its own. It is a command on the server and nowhere else: not in the web UI, not in the API, so that nobody with Admin in the application can do it by accident. It refuses to run while the service is up, takes a backup first, and asks for the word `reset`. It removes items, catalog entries, vendors, owners, tags, stored imports and the history of those records, and keeps accounts, groups, roles, tokens, settings, choices and lifecycle rules. With `--everything` it goes back to a fresh installation, accounts and audit trail included. This is the one way anything is deleted, and the backup holds what was. |
| D-75 | `/etc/hirdir/config.toml` is not a conffile from 0.3.0, so that no upgrade stops to ask about it and an unattended upgrade always goes through. The package ships its text as `/usr/share/hirdir/config.toml`. `hirdir-setup --settings`, which postinst runs at install and at each upgrade after the migrations, writes the file on a first install, and at an upgrade puts the settings the file does not have yet into it, each commented out at its default, also where a site has edited the file. Nothing a site wrote is changed or moved, and the file as it was is kept beside it. `hirdir-setup --upgrade PACKAGE.deb` installs a newer package through apt with no question left to answer, and plain `apt install` gives the same result. The way of the `texas2step` project, taken a step further: there the file is a conffile and a merge is run after apt has asked. (Was P-59, changed: the shipped file was to stay frozen.) |
| D-76 | The package that is published is built after its release notes are written, and `make package-test` is run on that package. `make deb` builds the documentation as well as the web UI. (Was P-56.) |
| D-77 | The setup tool is part of the package, never a second file to fetch. A site downloads the one `.deb`, installs it, and from then on may let `hirdir-setup` do its upgrades: `--check` asks GitHub whether a newer release is published, `--upgrade` fetches it, compares it with the checksum published beside it and installs it, and `--proxy URL` sends both requests through an enterprise proxy. An upgrade is begun by the tool that is installed and finished by the tool the new package brings, which the old one runs once apt is done, so what a later version needs done at an upgrade is in that version. A package that is already on the host is installed with `--upgrade PACKAGE.deb`, for a host with no way out. |
| D-78 | The package ships `hirdir-upgrade.timer`, which is off unless a site turns it on. Turned on, it runs `hirdir-setup --upgrade --yes` each night at a time of its own between 21:00 and 03:00, in the host's local time, and installs a newer release from GitHub when one is published. A night that was missed is not made up for by day. The proxy it goes through is `proxy` under `[upgrades]` in the configuration file. |
| D-79 | Tags are imported and exported like any other record, and come first in the load order. Choices, settings, lifecycle rules and the access records are not carried by files. (Was P-41.) |
| D-80 | A file is sent as the body of `POST /imports/preview`, with its entity, mode and name beside it in the URL, and not as a form upload. A file over the limit is a `413`. (Was P-42.) |
| D-81 | A column that an export writes and nobody can set, such as a `_source`, a detail of the catalog entry or `updated_at`, is accepted by an import and passed over. Only a column the entity does not know rejects the file. (Was P-44.) |
| D-82 | A bulk edit is a preview of the same kind as an import's: `POST /items/bulk-update` previews and stores it, `POST /items/bulk-update/{hid}/apply` applies it. It sets what an import may set, except, on more than one item at once, the fields that tell one item from another, and may add and remove tags. (Was P-47.) |
| D-83 | `fields` on a list names the fields of the record to return, as the route names them, with a dot for one field of a record within it. `hid` and `row_version` always come. (Was P-49.) |
| D-84 | A vendor carries `review_status`, as a catalog entry does. (Was P-50.) |
| D-85 | `make package-test` takes a backup before it imports `examples/` and restores it afterwards, with `hirdir admin backup` and `hirdir admin restore`. (Was P-57.) |
| D-86 | Scenario S18 is tried in 0.4.0 with the owner and the lifecycle state, which a minor item inherits from its parent from this release. Its location half joins it in 0.5.0. (Was P-60.) |
| D-87 | The replacement record and `POST /items/{hid}/replace` are in 0.4.0. A replacement links the item that left and the item that took its place. The new item takes over the current group memberships of the old one, with the same role and position: the old ones end and the new ones begin on the day of the replacement. Neither item is moved in the lifecycle by it. An item is replaced once and takes the place of one item, and never the place of an item that, in the end, took its own. Replacements in files and in reports stay with 0.6.0. (Was P-61.) Lifted in part in 0.9.8, at the owner's word of 2026-11-18: an item is replaced, and takes a place, more than once (P-241), and a replacement changes of either item what its request names (P-242). |
| D-88 | A minor item takes its lifecycle state, disposition and intent together. With a state of its own it has its own three. With none it has the three of the nearest item above it that has a state. The owner is inherited by itself. A minor item is returned to inheriting by a transition to no state, recorded like any other move. The state of a major item is never cleared. Sharpens P-07. (Was P-62.) |
| D-89 | Minor items nest at most three deep beneath a major item: a chassis, a blade, a module, a transceiver. `depth` is 0 for a major item and is a field to filter and sort by. (Was P-63.) |
| D-90 | The name of a device group is unique among groups. A group carries `record_status` and tags. Its type is not changed once it has had a member, and groups nest at most eight deep. A `custom` group takes the role `member`. An item is a current member of one group at most once, and of at most one stack and one cluster among the groups that are not retired. (Was P-64.) |
| D-91 | A stack has exactly one master among its current members, and may be its master alone: one that waits for its second switch in staging, or has lost a member in production. A stack with no members at all is accepted whatever its status, as a shell whose members are still to come, and a draft one may have no master yet. Members are added several at a time, by one request, and checked together. In a file of members the check is made once the file has been worked through, and the rows of a group it would leave wrong are rejected together. Sharpens D-21. (Was P-65, changed: an active stack needed at least two members.) **Changed by D-93:** a stack has at most one master, and may have none. |
| D-92 | An item has a condition, the state of the hardware as a person knows it: untested, good, degraded, suspect, failed or dead, from a list that can be edited. It is a field and not a tag, set by people and audited, and it holds while the item is on a shelf. A device group has a health that is worked out from its members when it is read and stored nowhere: a stack that is its master alone, or has a member that is not in good condition, is degraded, and says why. What is observed of an item or a group, online, degraded or unreachable, is a third fact, kept apart from the condition and from the intent. It is set by hand or by an import until a monitoring tool writes it, which waits for the integrations after 1.0.0. Built in 0.4.1. |
| D-93 | Which member of a stack is master is decided by the stack itself as it boots, and may move from one member to another over time, so it is an observation and not something a person keeps true by hand. The role `master` of a membership is the master that is intended, the member given the priority, and is optional: a stack has at most one and may have none, and marking another member unmarks the one that had it, in the same change. The master that is seen is kept apart, on the stack, with the moment it was seen: set by hand or by an import until a monitoring tool writes it, after 1.0.0. No rule depends on a master, and none stops a member leaving. The serial number of a stack is read from the observed master when one is recorded and otherwise from the intended one, says which, and is stored nowhere. A stack answers to the serial number of any of its current members. Where the observed master is not the intended one, the stack says so, and that is not a fault. Retiring a group ends its current memberships on that day. Changes D-21 and D-91 in what they say of the master. (Was P-66, changed: the master was required, and could not leave a stack that had other members.) |
| D-94 | A group has an owner of its own, optional. The group's record also lists, in `members_disagree`, each field of its own that a current member contradicts: for now `owner_hid`, when a member has another owner in force. The page shows the owner plainly when nobody disagrees and marks it when somebody does. (Was P-67.) |
| D-95 | In the counts: an item with no catalog entry is counted as hardware. A cluster comes before a stack: an item in both is part of the cluster's one managed node (**a cluster is one node only where it is managed as one: see D-96**). Only an active group makes its members one node, and only an active stack earns the stack discount; in a view that includes drafts a draft group does too, as a draft item is counted there. The discount is earned by the members of a stack that has two or more current members: one switch alone takes the full effort to look after, whatever it is called. Support weight is summed exactly and rounded to two places once, half up. Minor items are grouped under the owner and state in force for them. (Was P-68, changed: one switch alone in a stack earned the discount.) |
| D-96 | A cluster says how it is managed, in `managed_as`. `one`: the cluster is one device on the network and its members do not appear by themselves, as a pair of wireless LAN controllers in HA; it is one managed node. `members`: every member is managed at its own address and virtual addresses are shared between them, as a pair of firewalls in HA; it is as many managed nodes as it has members. `members_and_cluster`: every member is managed and the cluster answers as a virtual node as well; it is one more than its members. Left out, `one`, which is how 0.4.0 counted every cluster. A stack is always one managed node. The component count and the support weight do not depend on it. An item in both a stack and a cluster is part of the cluster's node only where the cluster is managed as `one`. Built in 0.4.1. Changes D-95 in what it says of a cluster. |
| D-97 | An address is removed outright, with an audit event that holds what it was, and so is a network that no address is under. A membership is ended with a date and never removed. Marking an address as the primary management address takes the mark off the one that had it, in the same change, and only an address with the role `management` may be primary. (Was P-69.) **Changed by D-98:** there is one primary of each family, IPv4 and IPv6, and marking one takes the mark off the one of its own family. |
| D-98 | The JSON form of a file carries the same columns as the CSV form. An item or a device group may have a primary management address of each family, IPv4 and IPv6, either or neither. `mgmt_ipv4` and `mgmt_ipv6` set them, `mgmt_address` takes an address of either family and files it under its own, and `mgmt_network`, the optional label of the network the addresses are under, goes with whichever the row sets; left out, they are in the unlabelled address space. The spellings a site's own file is likely to use are read as those columns: `ip_address`, `ip` and `mgmt_ip` as `mgmt_address`, `ipv4_address` and `ipv4` as `mgmt_ipv4`, `ipv6_address` and `ipv6` as `mgmt_ipv6`, so a first file of host names and addresses loads as it is. Every address of a record travels in the `addresses` entity, and an export writes the management columns only when asked for. A column that refers to an item, `parent` on an item and `item` on a membership or an address, takes the HID, or a serial number, an asset tag or a name that one item alone has. An export writes the asset tag when there is one, then the serial number, then the name, and the HID instead when another item answers to the same text. The rows of one file are worked an item before its minor items, and a group before the groups within it, wherever they stand. The file of addresses is loaded after the device groups, because a group has addresses too. Changes D-97 in what it says of the primary address. (Was P-70, changed: there was one management address to a record, in one column.) |
| D-99 | There are two reports of a view of the items, each taking the filter of the item list. The summary, `GET /reports/summary`, is for the people who run the network: items, component count, managed-node count and support weight, with the components counted by health and by condition, and no money. The management report, `GET /reports/management`, is for the people who pay for it: items, component count, estimated value and annual support cost, for the whole inventory and for each site, with no condition and no health. Each answers with the totals of the view and one row for each value of `by`. The summary is by `vendor`, `kind`, `type`, `function`, `owner`, `lifecycle_state`, `disposition`, `condition`, `health` or `tag`; the management report by `vendor`, `kind`, `type`, `function`, `owner` or `tag`, and by `site`, which is what it shows unless asked otherwise, from the release that has sites, 0.5.0. `region` and `contract` join both as those records arrive. An item with several tags is in the row of each, and the totals count it once. Built in 0.4.1. (Was P-71, changed: one report held all six measures, value and cost among them.) |
| D-100 | The record of an item lists its current memberships and its addresses, and for an endpoint of a controller domain its controllers: the items with the role `controller` in that domain, and the current members of every cluster nested in it. (Was P-72.) |
| D-101 | `GET /reports/duplicates` lists each address that is held more than once under one network, the unlabelled address space counting as one network, with the items and groups that hold it. A holder that a list leaves out by default is left out here, unless asked for in the same way. (Was P-73.) |
| D-102 | An address that two devices share, such as the virtual address of a pair of routers or firewalls, is an address of the group they are in, held once, and each member keeps its own management address: it is then no duplicate, and the duplicates report is left with the repeats nobody meant. The examples show a pair so. The controllers of an endpoint are found through the groups nested in its controller domain at any depth, so a domain may hold several clusters, and an endpoint that fails over between them is a member of the domain alone. Each controller is listed with the cluster it is in. Built in 0.4.1. Adds to D-100. |
| D-103 | Retiring an item ends its current memberships of groups that day, and so does a move to Released or to Archived: the item has left the team's hands, and is in no stack, cluster or domain any more. The memberships stay on record with the day they ended. Since D-93 no rule of leaving stands in the way: the master of a stack is retired like any other member. A move to any other state leaves memberships as they are, so a failed switch in Down or in Pending-Removal is still a member of its stack, which is what shows the stack as degraded. (Was P-74, changed: a move to Released or Archived left memberships as they were.) |
| D-104 | A change that is checked against other records takes the one writer's place before it looks: a membership, the item a minor item is in, a replacement, a group's status. Of two such requests at the same moment the second waits for the first, then sees what it stored and is answered accordingly. (Was P-75.) |
| D-105 | In the web UI the State, Disposition, Expected online and Owner columns of the item grid show, filter and sort by the value in force, so a minor item is found under what it has from the item above; the item's own state is a field of the filter box. The team mostly works with lists of major items, so the item grid leaves minor items out until a switch, Include minor items, is turned on; the switch is remembered for each person, as the layout of the columns is, and what is copied, exported or edited in bulk is what the grid shows. The columns that arrived with 0.4.0 are hidden until asked for. Health is shown from the start, as a mark in its colour; Condition and Observed status are hidden until asked for. The summary is under Reports, with its headline totals, the health counts among them and no money, on the first page; the management report has a tab of its own. Addresses with their networks have an entry of their own in the navigation. (Was P-76, with the health column and the minor items added by the answer.) |
| D-106 | The text of a GitHub release is the release's notes with each paragraph and list item on one line, and with links to other pages made whole, printed by `make release-body`. The file in `docs/releases/` stays wrapped like every other page. Sharpens P-11. (Was P-77, confirmed on 2026-11-13, when the four releases that were out had been given their text so and were looked at.) |
| D-107 | The fields of D-92. An item has `condition`, a value of the `item_condition` list, blank until somebody says. An item and a device group have `observed_status`, one of `online`, `degraded`, `unreachable` and `present`, and `observed_at`, which is the moment it was set unless a file gives another. `present` is what a person saw: the item is where it should be and in order, which is the only observation there will ever be of a fibre tester or of a power unit with no management connection, and `observed_at` then says when it was last checked. `health` is derived, for an item and for a stack or a cluster, as `good`, `degraded`, `failed` or `unknown`, with the reasons: the worst of what the condition says and what is observed against the intent, and for a group the worst of its members, of what is observed of the group itself, and of a member that is missing or is Down or in Pending-Removal. Observed `present` counts as observed `online` does. `untested` is unknown and not good; unreachable while not expected online is degraded and not failed; a stack that is one switch alone is degraded unless `expected_members` says one. A group has `expected_members`, optional, so that a stack whose failed switch has been taken out still shows as short of one. A controller domain and a custom group have no health. Neither the condition nor the health moves an item in the lifecycle. An item's health can be filtered and sorted by; a group's is shown and not yet filtered by. (Was P-78, with `present` added by the answer.) |
| D-108 | A row with neither serial number nor asset tag finds the one item, not retired, that has that name or that host name, whether or not the item has a serial number. A row that gives a name is matched on the item's name, or on its host name where the name is blank; a row that gives only a host name is matched on the item's host name. More than one item answering is an error on that row, never a guess. So a site's own file of host names, with what it observed of each or with their addresses, finds the items it has, and makes no second item beside one that has a serial number. Decided on 2026-11-14, when the review of 0.4.1 showed that such a file made duplicates. Changes D-71 and the fourth rule of matching. |
| D-109 | What the review of 0.4.1 found the decisions silent on. An item that is Released or Archived joins no group, as a retired one does not, and at the upgrade to 0.4.1 the memberships of such items that 0.4.0 had left standing are ended that day, each with an audit event. A person who states again what is observed, as it is, has looked again, and the moment is now; a file that states it again with no moment changes nothing, so that the same file loaded twice changes nothing. A moment is not cleared by itself, is not ahead of now by more than a day, and with no zone is in the application's time zone, the `time_zone` setting, which is America/New_York until it is changed: a moment is typed as it is read. A replacement hands over the role, the mark of the intended master with it, and not the master that was seen: nobody has seen the new item as master. One file names at most one intended and one observed master of a stack, and a second row that names one is rejected. `mgmt_network` is the network of both management addresses where they share one, and says nothing where they do not. Sorting by health puts the worst first. (Was P-79, decided on 2026-11-14 with one change: as built in 0.4.1 a moment with no zone was UTC.) |
| D-110 | A filter that compares a time with a value that names no zone, `observed_at:gt:2026-11-02T10:00`, reads the value in the application's time zone, as D-109 reads a moment that is entered. A value that names a zone, or ends in `Z`, is that moment. It was UTC. (Was P-80, decided on 2026-11-14 as built.) |
| D-111 | The item grid does not show Estimated value until it is asked for. It is a column like Annual support cost and Support weight: in the column chooser, and filtered, sorted and exported by as before. The columns shown at first are Name, Vendor, PID, Serial number, State, Disposition, Expected online, Health, Owner, Tags and LDoS (**and Operator, after Owner, from D-128**). A layout that a person has saved is as they saved it. Decided on 2026-11-14: after D-99 and D-105 the money is in the management report, and the grid's first columns are for what is there and how it is doing. |
| D-112 | A condition that a site adds to the `item_condition` list says nothing of health either way, as D-107 has it, and the reason that is given says so: "its condition is in-repair, which says nothing of its health, and nothing is observed". For `untested` it is "its condition is untested, and nothing is observed". It read "nothing is said of its condition", which was not so. The health is as it was: not known, unless what is observed decides it. Letting each condition of the list carry a meaning for health was the other answer, and is left until a site asks for it. Decided on 2026-11-14. |
| D-113 | In the examples, NetConfig Manager is one licence, of quantity 1, that says in its description that it is for 250 devices. Its catalog entry's support weight and annual support cost are those of the whole licence. It had quantity 250, which D-38 multiplied into 3,000,000 a year, nearly all of the examples' annual support cost in the management report. Decided on 2026-11-14. The examples no longer hold a quantity that is multiplied; the tests do. |
| D-114 | A region carries `record_status`, as a site, a location and a contact do. A region, a site or a location is retired only when nothing that is not retired is in it: no region, site or location beneath it, and no item or device group placed there. `code` is optional, and unique when present: among regions, among sites, and within its site for a location. A name holds no `>`, which separates the names of a path, and within one region a site and a region directly beneath it do not share a name, so that a path names one record. Locations nest at most eight deep within a site, and a location stays in the site it was made in. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-81.) |
| D-115 | A region may hold a shipping address, the `ship_` set, and no street address. The six fields of a shipping address are inherited together, from the nearest record that has one, and `ship_instructions` by itself, from the nearest record that has it. Nothing is typed twice: a site or a location whose deliveries go to its street address says so with one flag, `ship_same_as_address`, and its shipping address is then its street address, read as it is asked for and stored nowhere else, for itself and for every place beneath it. The flag and a shipping address of the place's own are not both given, and the flag needs a street address. Without the flag a street address is never taken for a shipping address. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-82, changed: the street address had to be typed again as the shipping address.) |
| D-116 | What is inherited is seen to be inherited, at a glance, on a page and in a form alike: a value that comes from a level above is shown apart from one that is the record's own, in a lighter style and with the level it came from. A value that is the record's own where a level above has one too is marked as an override, and says what it overrides. The answer of `.../effective` carries both: the source of each thing, and the level that it overrides. So a shipping address set once on a region is seen to reach the sites that share it, and the one site with another destination is seen to override it. Decided on 2026-11-14, with D-115. |
| D-117 | A contact assignment is removed outright, and so is a member of an owner, each with an audit event that holds what it was, as an address is. A contact is assigned once to one place in one role. "None here, and do not look further up" is an assignment with no contact and `stop_inheritance` set: it stands alone for its place and role, and an assignment with a contact never carries the flag. `priority` left out is one more than the highest there is for that place and role. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-83.) |
| D-118 | What a place has in force is answered for all three levels: `GET /regions/{hid}/effective` and `GET /sites/{hid}/effective` beside `GET /locations/{hid}/effective`. The answer has one entry for each role of the `contact_role` list, with the level it came from, and the shipping address and instructions with theirs. Since D-116 it also says what each of them overrides. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-84.) |
| D-119 | What is said of an owner outside the team is said where that owner is defined, once: `exception_reason` on the owner, which an external owner cannot be without (R7.5). An item and a device group hold no reason of their own. `owner_exception` is derived on both, true when the owner in force is external, so the minor items of such an item are marked too, and `owner_exception_reason` beside it is the reason of that owner, read as it is asked for and set nowhere but on the owner. Scenario S28 reads accordingly: an owner outside the team with no reason is refused, and with one it is accepted and the item given to it is marked as an exception. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-85, changed: the reason was a field of the item, asked for each time an item was given to such an owner.) |
| D-120 | The members of an owner are under `/owners/{hid}/members`. An owner may have none. One that has any has exactly one `primary`: the first member is it, marking another makes the one that had it an `alternate` in the same change, and the primary is not removed while others remain. Owners, regions, sites, locations and contacts take tags, as R1.9 asks. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-86.) |
| D-121 | `GET /geography/tree` answers the regions, their sites and the locations within them, each with `path`, `items` and `component_count` of everything at or beneath it, in the view that `filter`, `include_drafts` and `include_archived` choose. `root` names one region, site or location to start from, and `locations=false` stops at the sites. Beside the tree stand `totals`, `unplaced`, the items with no location in force, and `hidden`, the items in places the view leaves out. `site` and `region` join `by` of both reports. A row of a region holds everything at or beneath it, so an item is in the row of each region above it and the totals count it once; a row is labelled with its path. `GET /reports/management` with no `by` is by site. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-87.) |
| D-122 | An item and a device group have an operator: the one person of the team who looks after it, its steward, apart from its owner, which is the part of the organization that answers for it and stays as D-120 has it. The operator of a device group is the operator of the members that have none of their own. Decided on 2026-11-14: the team needs a way to assign devices to its named members, because a device that nobody is assigned to is one that nobody feels responsible for. The particulars are P-90. |
| D-123 | Files carry `regions`, `sites`, `locations`, `contacts`, `contact_assignments` and `owner_members`, the last loaded after the owners. A region is matched by its path, `parent` and `name`; a site by `region` and `name`; a location by `site`, `parent` and `name`. A contact is matched by its email address, which is unique when present, and otherwise by a name that one contact alone has. `location` on an item and on a device group takes a path or an HID. On the item list `location` takes `within`, with the HID or the path of a region, a site or a location, and `site` and `region` filter and sort by their names; `location` on the list of groups and of locations and `region` on the list of sites take `within` too. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-88.) **Changed by D-124** in the load order: contacts and operators come before the regions. |
| D-124 | An operator is a record of its own: a short label of the site's choosing, initials, a surname or a code such as `Op2`, with a colour, that stands for the member of the team who is assigned behind it. Items, device groups and places are given the label, never the person, and lists and reports show the label as a mark that leads to the person. The person behind a label is changed in one place, for a new hire or when the team swaps its assignments, and everything the label is on follows at once, with nothing written to any of it. A label with nobody behind it, because the person is away or has left, is an open assignment: what it is on still shows it, marked as open. A label that is retired is released: it stands for nothing, and each thing it was on has the operator of the next level up, or none. A region, a site and a location may be given an operator too, which an item there has when neither it nor a group it is in has one, so that a new device is never nobody's. Decided on 2026-11-14. Changes D-122 in what an operator is, which was a contact, and D-123 in the load order. The particulars are P-90. |
| D-125 | An item has a planned location beside the location it is at: where it is going, in a place that is not in use yet. A team that plans a new site makes the site as a draft, and gives the spares that are meant for it their planned locations there. The spares stay where they are, in staging, and are counted there, and the draft site shows what is planned for it. When the site is made active, every item planned for a location that is thereby in use is moved in by that same change: its location becomes the planned one and the plan is cleared, each item with an entry of its own in its history, and nobody moves anything device by device. The move is a recorded change of each item and not a reading: where an item is, is a fact about it, and its history says the day it changed. Decided on 2026-11-14, in answer to what P-89 said of a draft place. The particulars are P-92. |
| D-126 | What the review of 0.5.0 found the pages silent on. Regions nest at most sixteen deep, where the requirements said to any depth. A place is not brought back while what it is in is retired, and an item or a device group is not brought back into a location that was retired after it: it is given another location, or none, with the change. An item that has left the team's hands, Released or Archived, holds no location open. A draft place may hold records that are not drafts, though what is only meant for it has it as its planned location (D-125): the tree leaves the place out and counts what is there as hidden, and the reports, which are of items, give the place its row. A level whose only contact for a role is retired still supplies that role, with the contact marked as retired. In a file, the rows that name the primary of an owner are worked first; a row of a contact with no email address finds the one contact of that name, retired or not, and of several the one that has no email address. `hirdir admin reset` clears the records of this release with the rest of the inventory. The people of an owner are listed as a page, like the members of a device group. `region` of an item is the name of the region its site is in, in a filter, a sort and an export alike. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built, the draft place as D-125 has it. (Was P-89.) |
| D-127 | A device group has a planned location just as an item has: where the group is going, in a place that is not in use yet, and it is moved in by the change that makes the place active, with an entry of its own in the audit trail. **Changed by D-129:** the members of a group that have no location and no plan of their own have the group's, and go with it. Decided on 2026-11-14. Changes P-92, which had a device group with no planned location. |
| D-128 | The particulars of D-122 and D-124. An operator has a `label`, unique and at most 40 characters, a `description`, a `colour`, the `contact_hid` of the person behind it, which may be blank, and `record_status`. It is open, `is_open`, when it has no contact or its contact is retired. `operator_hid` on an item, a device group, a region, a site and a location names an operator. For an item the operator in force is resolved when it is read, the nearest first: its own; for a minor item, that of the nearest item above it that has one; then that of a group which the item at the top of that line is a current member of *(replaced by D-242, which was P-175: an item's own groups first, then the item it is in and that item's groups, up the line)*, a stack before a cluster before a controller domain before any other group, and where that group has none, that of the nearest group it is nested in; then that of the location the item is at, of the locations above it, of its site, and of each region outward. A retired operator is passed over at every level. The answer says where it came from: `own`, `parent_item`, `device_group`, `location`, `site` or `region`, with the record. Both reports take `by=operator`, and the blank row is what nobody is assigned to; on the item list `effective.operator_hid:empty:true` finds it and `operator.is_open:eq:true` what is assigned to a label with nobody behind it, and the first page of the web UI says how many components each is. In a file the column `operator` holds the label, the operators are a file of their own, loaded after the contacts, and a row that names the operator an item has anyway leaves it inheriting. A bulk edit sets it on many items at once. The Operator column of the item grid is shown from the start, after Owner: D-111's list of first columns gains it. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built, with one change: the Operator column is shown from the start, where it was hidden until asked for. (Was P-90.) |
| D-129 | Inheritance flows down through each hierarchy, throughout, with an override at every level: what is set once on the record above is had by the records beneath it that say nothing of their own. So a device with no location of its own is where its device group is, and one with no planned location of its own is planned for where its group is planned for, as it has its group's operator: an operator puts devices into a group and moves the group, and does not go back to each device. Decided on 2026-11-14. The particulars are D-134. |
| D-130 | An item with no owner of its own, and none from an item above it, has the owner of its device group, in the order and in the way of D-129, and a group with none has that of the group it is nested in. Whether what an item or a group has is an exception with an owner outside the team goes by the owner in force. On an upgrade nothing that is held changes, and the counts by owner do: an item that was nobody's, in a group that has an owner, is counted with that owner from then on. A group still says where a member contradicts it: a member with another owner of its own, or from the item it is in. Decided on 2026-11-14. Settles what P-93 left open of the owner. |
| D-131 | A scoreboard, under Reports: who looks after how much, and where. The operators, each with the items, the components, the managed nodes and the support weight it is in force for, the most first, with what nobody is assigned to and what is under an open label beside them; and the sites in the same way, each with the operators involved there and how much each has. It is the summary by `operator` and by `site`, and `operators=true` on `GET /reports/summary` adds to each row the operators in force among its items, each with its items, components and support weight, the most first and nobody last. The tables are in that order at first, and are sorted by any column by its header. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built, with the headers made sortable, where the order was fixed. (Was P-91.) |
| D-132 | Every list and every table that the web UI shows is sorted by its headers: a click on a header sorts by that column, and another turns the order round. A grid that the server pages is sorted by the server, over the whole list and never over the page on show alone, so every column of such a grid has a field that the server sorts by. A table that holds all of its rows is sorted in the browser. Text sorts without regard to case, numbers as numbers, dates as dates, and blanks last. A table of one row, and a layout of labels and values that is no table, have nothing to sort. Decided on 2026-11-14. |
| D-133 | The particulars of D-125. `planned_location_hid` on an item names a location that is not in use: the location itself, a location above it, its site or a region above that is a draft. A location that is in use is refused as a plan, since the item can simply be moved there, and so is a retired one. A place is in use when it and everything it is in is active. Whenever a region, a site or a location is made active, by a change, by a file or by being brought back, every item that is not retired and is planned for a location now in use is moved, in the same transaction and under the same request ID. A plan is given up by clearing it, and is cleared by itself when the item is given the planned location as its location. Giving the item another location in the meantime leaves the plan standing. A location is not retired while items are planned for it. A device group has a planned location in the same way (D-127), which its members have that have none of their own (D-129). `planned_location` is a column of a file and of a bulk edit, by path, and a field with `within` on the lists of items and of groups; the tree of places says for each node how many items and how many groups are planned for it or beneath it. A draft place is so a workspace for a project: made under the name of the project beside the place it replaces, filled with what is new and planned for by what is live, made active in one change, and given the real name once the old place is retired and renamed out of the way, a retired place keeping its name until then. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-92.) |
| D-134 | The particulars of D-129. The location in force of an item is, the nearest first: its own; for a minor item, that of the nearest item above it that has one; then that of a group which the item at the top of that line is a current member of *(replaced by D-242, which was P-175: an item's own groups first, then the item it is in and that item's groups, up the line)*, a stack before a cluster before a controller domain before any other group, and where that group has none, that of the nearest group it is nested in. The planned location in force is resolved in the same order. The answer says where each came from, `own`, `parent_item` or `device_group`, with the record. When a place is made active, what has a plan of its own is moved in as D-125 has it, and what has a location of its own but its plan from above, from its group or from the item it is in, gives its own location up in the same change and is thereafter where that group or item is: it has gone with it. A member with a location of its own while its group has another is an override and no disagreement: the access points of a controller domain are each where they hang, and the item says where its location came from. A group nested in another has the location and the plan of the group it is in, and the lists of items and of groups filter, sort and count by what is in force. The owner is had from a group in the same way, by D-130. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-93.) |
| D-135 | Making a place active is an intent, and is never a change of a field that ripples through the inventory unannounced. Before it is made, whoever makes it is shown what it brings about: how many items and how many device groups move in, and how many records are written to. `GET /regions/{hid}/opening`, and the same for a site and a location, answers that and changes nothing; while a place above is not active it says which, and that nothing moves until then. The web UI opens a place only by its **Make active** action, which shows that answer and asks; the form of a draft place offers no record status. A file that makes a place active says the same in the notes of its row in the preview, before it is applied. The API does what it is told, as it does everywhere: a client that changes `record_status` itself asks `opening` first if it wants to know. Decided on 2026-11-14. |
| D-136 | The particulars of D-132 on the server. A column of several values is sorted by the least of them, in the order of that value, and a record with none comes last either way: the tags of any record, the groups, group types, addresses and networks of an item or a device group, the aliases of a vendor, the groups of a user, the holders of a duplicate address. An address is sorted as an address and not as text, every IPv4 address before any IPv6 one, and the management address of a record is its primary one, the IPv4 one before the IPv6 one. A count is sorted as a number, counted as the record counts it; the minor items of an item that has none are blank, as the cell is, and last. The health of a device group is sorted the worst first, as an item's is by D-109, with a group that has no health last, and is still not filtered by (D-107). The role of a user is sorted from the lowest to the highest, a user with none last, and the member that is seen as master comes first whichever way round. A field that is worked out for a column, `mgmt_address`, `minor_items`, `serial_number`, `health`, `master.name`, `observed_master.name` and `member_count` of a device group, `uses`, `aliases` and `primary.name`, is sorted by and not filtered by. The tables that are paged and are no list, `/users`, `/audit-events`, `/imports` and the rows of one, `/reports/duplicates`, the members of a group and the minor items and the history of an item, take `sort` over the columns their pages show; the order each had is what it has with no `sort`, and what settles the rows a sort leaves tied. Three columns are not sorted by the server: What changed of the audit trail, and Changes and Reasons of the rows of a preview. Each is text that the page composes from a list of changes, and a sort by anything the database holds would not be a sort by what the cell shows. The table is in [interfaces.md](interfaces.md#sorting). Decided on 2026-11-14 as built: the three columns that a page composes from a list are not sorted by the server. (Was P-94.) |
| D-137 | A contract is one record that is applied to one item or to many, and what is said of it is said there, once. An item under it holds nothing of the contract but the link: the number, the period, the status and the support level it shows are the contract's, read as they are asked for. When a contract is renewed it is usually given a new number and a new period of performance: that is a change of the one record, and every item in scope has the new number and the new period at once, with nothing written to any of them. The items in scope are those whose coverage under the contract has not been ended. Decided on 2026-11-14, while 0.6.0 was being built. The particulars are P-106. |
| D-138 | A contract says how it was bought and whom to ask. `purchase_reference` is the request or the tracking number its present term was bought under, an RITM, a purchase order, a procurement number, and each earlier term keeps its own, so the terms of a contract are its purchase history. `vendor_hid` is the manufacturer or the provider, and `reseller_hid`, optional, the vendor it was bought through. `account_manager` and `support_contact` are free text, a name with a telephone number or an address, as many lines as are wanted: no contact record is made for a vendor's desk. `support_url` is where support is reached online, and `notes` holds whatever else. Decided on 2026-11-14, while 0.6.0 was being built: free text is enough for now, and structured contacts of a contract are added if they come to be asked for. |
| D-139 | Above the contracts there may be a support agreement: an enterprise agreement or enterprise support agreement between the organization and a vendor, which covers some part of the inventory through support contracts whose numbers are linked to it. The contracts are grouped into the agreement, and what the agreement says of whom to ask and of the related details is had by each contract in it that says nothing of its own, and overridden by one that does: set once, inherited, overridden where needed, as everywhere. So there is a Cisco agreement with several SMARTnet contract numbers under it and a Juniper one, while a few switches bought lately are covered by a contract that stands by itself and may be rolled into the agreement later, which is one change of that contract. Decided on 2026-11-14, while 0.6.0 was being built. The particulars are P-107. |
| D-140 | A contract carries `record_status` beside its `status`, and takes tags. `status` is what a person says of the contract, `active`, `expired`, `pending` or `cancelled`, and is never changed by the clock. `number` is unique with the vendor, without regard to case. Its dates may be blank: no start is "from the beginning", no end is "until further notice". A contract covers on a day when it is not retired and not a draft, its `status` is `active`, and the day is within its period. `annual_cost` is the contract's own total and is added to nothing: the annual support cost of a report is still that of the items. A contract whose period has ended while its status still says `active` has lapsed, and is flagged: `lapsed` on the contract, worked out when it is read, a field to filter the list of contracts by, a mark on the Contracts page, and a count on the coverage report that leads to the list, so that somebody renews it or says that it has expired. An agreement is flagged in the same way. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-95, with the flag added by the answer.) |
| D-141 | A coverage is one item under one contract for a range of days, `starts_on` and `ends_on`, each of which is the contract's where it is blank. It ends in one of three ways. It is ended by giving it `ends_on`, and stays on record. It is ended by `hirdir` itself on the day its item is retired or moved to Released or to Archived, with an audit event of its own, and is then marked `left_service`: the item has left, and the vendor still counts it until somebody says so. Or, where it was a mistake, an item put under the wrong contract, it is removed outright by `DELETE`, with an audit event that holds what it was, as an address is. Under one contract an item has no two coverages whose days overlap. Coverage is added several items at a time, by one request. A minor item may be covered by itself. An item is `covered` when a coverage of it is in force today, in the application's time zone; `covered_until` is the last day any of its coverages under a contract of status `active` runs to, blank when one of them names no end, and a gap between two of them is not looked for. Both, and `contract.number`, are fields of the item list. The coverage report lists as out of service every item that is out of service and still covered, and every item whose coverage was ended because it left while the term of its contract still runs: the list to send the vendor. A coverage drops off that list when the contract is renewed into a new period, or when a person clears `left_service` because the vendor has been told. Scenario S29 reads accordingly. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-96, changed by the answer: a coverage was never removed, and nothing ended one but a person.) |
| D-142 | An item is in service when the lifecycle state in force for it is one of the `service_states` setting: Assigned, Test, Prod and Down unless changed. Gear that has been handed to the team and is parked, a spare or staging stock, is so in service: an uncovered spare is a gap. It is out of service when it is retired or its state in force is Pending-Removal, Released or Archived. An item with no state, or one that is Purchased or Delivered, is neither. `GET /reports/coverage` counts the major items in service, how many are covered and how many are not, the items out of service that a contract still counts (D-141), and the items in service whose cover runs out within `within_days`, 90 unless asked otherwise. Minor items are left out of what is uncovered, because a power supply is covered with its switch. Each of the three is a list of items: `section` asks for a page of it, and the answer gives for each the filter that lists the same items on the item list, so that a count leads to its rows and to an export of them. The report is of today. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-97, changed by the answer: Assigned was not among the states of service.) |
| D-143 | `GET /reports/end-of-life` is of one of the four dates, `date`, which is `ldos` unless asked otherwise, by the date in force. It counts the items and the components of the view whose date is past, and whose date is from today to `within_days` ahead, 730 unless asked otherwise, for each of the four dates; `section`, `past` or `approaching`, asks for a page of the items of the chosen date, the soonest first; and `by_fiscal_year` has one row for each fiscal year that a date of the view falls in. A fiscal year starts on the first day of `fiscal_year_start_month` and is named for the calendar year it ends in. It holds no money: the management report takes the same filter. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-98.) |
| D-144 | `GET /vendors/{hid}/portfolio` answers the totals of what the vendor makes or provides, items, components, support weight, estimated value and annual support cost, with how many of the items are covered; one row for each of the four kinds, also where there is nothing of a kind; one row for each model, with its dates, its items, how many of them are covered and the day the first of their covers runs out; the agreements with the vendor; the contracts the vendor provides or that were bought through it, by the reseller in force; and the capacity lines that name the vendor. The items themselves are the item list with `catalog.vendor_hid`, and the file that `hirdir vendors portfolio` writes is an export of them with the columns of the portfolio. It takes `include_drafts` and `include_archived` as a list does. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-99.) |
| D-145 | A purchase request has no `record_status`: its `status` has `draft` and `cancelled`. A line is numbered within its request, `line`, from 1, and is named `RITM0012345/2`; `quantity` is never below what was received, and a line is taken back by lowering it to that. `received_quantity` is the number of items that name the line, counted when it is read and stored nowhere, and `pending_quantity` what is left. `POST /purchase-lines/{hid}/receive` takes the items that arrived, each with a serial number, an asset tag or a name, and may take a `quantity` beside them or instead of them: so many more arrived that nothing is known of yet. Each of those is created with a name made from its line and its place on it, `RITM0012345/2-003`, to be given its serial number later, by hand or by a file that names it so. All are created in Delivered, of the line's model, each naming the line, in one change, with the day and the location where those are given. More than is left is refused, and so is receiving on a request that is a draft or cancelled. The status of a request is set by people, with one exception: once every line of a request has arrived in full, a request that is `pending` or `ordered` becomes `received` by that same change, with an audit event. A person may also say `received` earlier, because a request may hold lines that this inventory does not follow. `GET /purchase-lines` lists the lines of every request, with `pending_quantity` to filter by: that is the planning list of R5.3. An item's `purchase_line_hid` may be set by hand, and in a file `purchase_line` takes the name of a line. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-100, changed by the answer: every item received had to be identified, and no delivery changed the status of a request.) |
| D-146 | An external system carries `record_status` and is retired, not deleted; `kind` is one of a fixed list *(replaced by D-237, which was P-170: a value of the editable list `system_kind`)*. An item or a group is registered with one system once: a registration that ends is given the status `removed`, and one that begins again is the same record. One that was made by mistake is removed outright by `DELETE /registrations/{hid}`, with an audit event that holds what it was. A registration is on the record it was made for: that of a stack is on the stack, and is not one of the registrations of its members. So that nobody takes a member for a device no tool knows, the record of an item also lists, in `group_registrations` and to be read only, what a stack or a cluster it is a current member of is registered with, each with the group. `confirmed_at` is a moment as `observed_at` is: with no zone it is in the application's time zone, and it is not more than a day ahead. `registration.system` on the list of items finds an item by a system that knows it, by a registration of its own or of a stack or a cluster it is a current member of, a registration that is `removed` not counting, so `registration.system:ne:NetWatch` lists what that system does not know; on the list of device groups it finds a group by its own. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-101, changed by the answer: a registration was never removed, and a member showed nothing of what its stack is registered with.) |
| D-147 | The `capacity_category` list is loaded with its six defaults by 0.6.0. A demand line has the category `other_work` and a supply line any other of the list. The name of a line is unique on its side. `quantity` and `units_each` have two places, and the units of a line are their product, exactly. A line is in date on a day that is not before `starts_on` and not after `ends_on`, by the application's day. `GET /reports/capacity` is of the whole installation: a line's region or site is kept and shown, and narrows nothing yet. A report of one region or one site is designed once the report of the whole has been used. It answers the balance twice, `active` and `with_drafts`, each with the support weight, the infrastructure demand, the other demand, the supply in total and by category, the balance, the coverage as a percentage, blank when there is no demand, and the share of the supply that is staff. Every number is worked out exactly and rounded to two places once, half up. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-102.) |
| D-148 | Replacements travel in files as `item_replacements`: `old_item`, `new_item`, `reason`, `reference`, `replaced_on`. A row is matched by its HID or by the item that left, and is loaded after the members of groups, so that the memberships a file already shows as handed over are not handed over twice. A row that names another new item for an item already replaced is rejected. `GET /reports/replacements` is the list of them, filtered, sorted and paged as a list is, with the count of each reason beside it. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-103.) |
| D-149 | `contract` joins `by` of the summary and of the management report: an item is in the row of each contract that covers it today, as an item with several tags is in the row of each, and the blank row is what no contract covers. A row is labelled with the number of the contract and the name of its vendor. An item that more than one contract covers today is worth a look before a renewal: two contracts for one device are usually one too many, and only one of them is renewed, though there are honest exceptions, such as one vendor for technical support and another for hardware replacement. So the item says how many contracts cover it today, `contract_count`, a field to filter and sort the item list by, and the coverage report has a fourth list, `overlapping`, of the items that more than one contract covers, each with its contracts. It is a list to look at and not a fault. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-104, with the list of items under more than one contract added by the answer.) |
| D-150 | The particulars of D-137. `POST /contracts/{hid}/renew` takes the new `number`, `starts_on` and `ends_on`, and with them `annual_cost` and `purchase_reference`, and `support_level`, `reseller_hid` and `notes` where those change too; it is one change of the contract, recorded as `renew`, it makes the status `active` unless it is told another, and it is refused when it gives neither a new number nor a new period. **A renewal is often entered before the old term ends, and the old term then runs on.** A renewal whose period starts after today is kept as the next term of the contract, `next_term` on its record: the present number and period stay in force until the day the new one starts, and from that day the contract has the new number and the new period, worked out when it is read, with no change that the clock made. The items under it are covered throughout, `covered_until` runs to the end of the next term, and nothing is written to any of them. A next term is withdrawn by a renewal that says so. An agreement is renewed in the same way, `POST /agreements/{hid}/renew`, and the contracts that are co-termed with it follow. **A renewal is also often late.** Procurement may take thirty to ninety days after a term has ended, and in that time the vendor still gives support, by escalation: the account manager is asked before the support line is called. A person says so with the status `renewal_pending`: while it holds, the contract covers past the end of its period, its items stay off the list of what no contract covers and say that the renewal is pending, and the coverage report counts the contracts and the items in that state, so that the limbo is seen. A contract whose period has ended and that says nothing of the kind has lapsed (D-140). A coverage follows its contract in each date that it has none of its own for, so a date that is given to a coverage and is the contract's own is kept as blank. A coverage with an end date of its own that has passed is out of scope and stays ended. The terms a contract had before, each with its number, its period, its cost and the moment it was renewed, are read from the audit trail and answered by `GET /contracts/{hid}/terms`; they are stored nowhere else. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-106, changed by the answer: a renewal replaced the period at once, also when the new one had not begun, and there was no status for a renewal that is late.) |
| D-151 | The particulars of D-139. An agreement has a `name`, unique, such as "Cisco Enterprise Agreement", an optional `number` of its own, the `vendor_hid` it is with, and the fields a contract has: `reseller_hid`, `starts_on`, `ends_on`, `status`, `support_level`, `purchase_reference`, `account_manager`, `support_contact`, `support_url`, `annual_cost`, `notes`, `record_status` and tags. `agreement_hid` on a contract puts it into one, and is blank on a contract that stands by itself; a contract is grouped into an agreement with its own vendor, and one that is created in an agreement and names no vendor has the agreement's. A contract in an agreement has from it, where its own is blank: the reseller, the support level, the account manager, the support contact, the support address and the period, `starts_on` and `ends_on`, each by itself. Its number, its status, its purchase reference, its cost and its notes are its own. The answer says where each came from, `own`, `agreement` or `none`, in `effective`, and a file that holds the very value a contract has from its agreement leaves it inheriting. So a contract with no dates of its own is co-termed with its agreement, and giving the agreement a new period moves every such contract, and every item under them, in one change. A change of an agreement's dates that would leave a contract in it with a period that ends before it starts is refused, and the answer names those contracts, whose own dates are put right first. Rolling a contract into an agreement leaves what the contract says of its own as overrides, to be cleared where the agreement should speak. The status of an agreement is what a person says of it and gates nothing: a contract covers by its own status. An agreement is retired once no contract that is not retired is in it, and a contract is not brought back while its agreement is retired, as a place is not while what it is in is retired. Files carry `agreements`, loaded before the contracts, and the portfolio of a vendor lists its agreements. Decided on 2026-11-14. (Was P-107, with the refusal added by the answer, after the review of 0.6.0 showed that a shortened agreement left a contract that no change could be made to.) |
| D-152 | How many items a contract covers, `covered_items`, and how many records are registered with an external system, `registrations`, are counts like every other: drafts, retired records and items in Archived are left out, so the number on a contract is the number of rows of the item list for that contract. The coverage of a contract still lists every coverage, with the status of its item. Decided on 2026-11-14, when the review of 0.6.0 showed that a contract over one active, one draft and one retired item said three. |
| D-153 | Files carry `agreements`, `contracts`, `contract_coverage`, `purchase_requests`, `purchase_lines`, `external_systems`, `registrations`, `capacity_lines` and `item_replacements`. The load order gains them: external systems after the networks, then agreements, contracts, purchase requests and their lines, the items, the groups, their members and the replacements, the addresses, the coverage, the registrations and the capacity lines. A coverage is matched by its contract, its item and the day it starts; a registration by its system and its item or group; a capacity line by its side and its name. `hirdir admin reset` clears all of them with the rest of the inventory. Decided on 2026-11-14 as built. (Was P-105.) |
| D-154 | An item that arrived as a count is given its serial number or its asset tag by a file that names it as its line named it. A row with a serial number or an asset tag that no item has, and the name of an item that is on a purchase line and has neither, is that item; the same row loaded again finds it by the serial number it gave. It is the one exception to "a row with a serial number that no item has is a new item", and is as narrow as the promise of D-145: every other such row is a new item, as before. Decided on 2026-11-14, when the second review of 0.6.0 showed that such a file made a second item beside the first. (Was P-108.) |
| D-155 | A renewal that is still to begin leaves the status of the present term as it is. On the day the new term begins, a contract or an agreement that said `expired` or `renewal_pending` is `active`, as it is after a renewal that is in force at once: both were said of the term that has ended.
