A context that is given a host, by whatever way, holds no serial number from then on: what it held is cleared in the same change and it shows its host's, so that a context moved to another switch shows that switch's, with nothing to blank. The row itself is found by the tool's identifier (P-248), its HID, its asset tag, or its name or host name, as a row that gives no serial number is. A virtual machine and a container keep a serial number of their own, where they have one. | A review of 0.9.8 loaded a tool's list of three contexts of one chassis, each with the chassis's serial number: the first row made a second record of a context that was there, with the chassis's serial number stored as its own, and the other two were rejected as naming the same record. Asked what a serial number given for a context should do, the owner, on 2026-11-18: "A new VDC imported could match an existing switch serial and be automtically linked to the existing switch, yes? VDC should be allowed to exist without a serial number, but when listed with a serial number it should indicate what switch it is tied, such as when discovered. If the VDC is discovered first with a serial and no switch exists yet with matching serial, what happens? VDC is created as an item. Later, the switch is discovered and created as an item. Is a later discovery going to join the VDC that was learned to the switch that was discovered after? Serial number isn't needed for VDC but it may help assign a VDC to a switch. If a VDC is moved to a switch with a different VDC, blank the serial number today is how that is handled or something different?" That the join waits for the next report or a person, and is not made the moment the switch is entered, was the lead's reading, by the owner's rule of 2026-11-18 that what follows a change is worked from reports in batches and not by a hook at each change (P-223), and the owner agreed to it the same evening: "it would take a second discovery to connect the VDC to the switch, so if VDC is discovered first, it has no switch, then switch is discovered, no link yet between the two, next discovery VDC is listed it now matches with the switch and the link is established, moving the VDC to the switch." | 0.9.8 |
| P-252 | **What runs on a stack runs on the stack, and not on a member of it.** A stack is one device: a container on a stack of switches runs on whichever member is active, and people reach for the stack. So a current member of a stack is not the host of anything. Where a request names as a host an item that is a current member of a stack, by the page, a file, the API, a bulk edit or the serial number of a virtual context (P-251), the host is that stack, and the member is what the item was seen on, with the moment of the change, unless the request says what it was seen on itself; the answer and a preview say so, and the web's field for a host offers the stack and shows a member as part of it. A switch that joins a stack while something runs on it hands what runs on it to the stack in the same change, and so does a switch that is made a stack (P-243), with the name and the addresses; the forms say how many before they are confirmed, and each item handed over has an event of its own that names the stack. A member that leaves a stack, is replaced or is retired takes nothing with it: what runs on the stack stays with the stack, which is not retired while something runs on it (P-247). A cluster is as it was: a virtual machine runs on the cluster as a whole or on one host of it, and one host retired leaves the cluster's machines with the cluster. | The owner, on 2026-11-18, while 0.9.8 was reviewed: "Can a container and/or virtual machine Run On a stack? If a switch is a member of a stack, can a container or virtual machine ron on the member switch? How is that handled as-is. This may need an update." As built both could be named, and they behaved apart: what ran on a member was listed on the member's page and not on the stack's, was retired with the member, and went to the spare pool with a member that left; and a switch made a stack kept what ran on it. The owner then cited the vendor's documentation of application hosting on Catalyst switches, "On all Cisco Catalyst stack and stackwise virtual models (all software versions), Guest Shell and the AppGigabitEthernet interface operate only on the active switch in the stack" (https://www.cisco.com/c/en/us/td/docs/ios-xml/ios/prog/configuration/26x/26x-programmability-cg/application-hosting.html), and decided: "We shoould keep the containers with the stack not the member switch." That it holds for a virtual machine and a virtual context as for a container was the lead's reading, which the owner confirmed the same evening: "Apply the rule that containers, virtual machines and virtual device contexts can run on a stack, not just containers". That a member named as a host stands for its stack, with the member as what the item was seen on, where the lead had first put that it be refused, so that a tool's report, which knows the switch and not the stack, still loads, was the lead's reading as well, and the owner agreed to it: "tool observes container on a member as host, hirdir puts the container on the stack". | 0.9.8 |
| P-253 | **Like columns stand in one sequence on every list.** Where a list has them, the columns of a place stand from the smallest outward, Location, Site, Region, and then Planned location; after them the people, Operator and then Owner; and Tags before Record status. That holds in the column chooser of every list and in the columns a list shows from the start. So the item list, the items of a vendor and the report of the end of life show Location before Site, where 0.9.5 had Site first; the list of device groups has its places in that order and before its operator and owner, where it had the owner first; the capacity lines have Site before Region; and the catalog has Tags before Record status. What a list is about keeps its place at the front: the catalog and the purchase lines begin with the vendor, and a circuit has its carrier before its owner. The seven columns the owner asked to see at 1280 pixels are still in view, Location now before Site. As built: a layout that a person's browser has kept for a list, from the first time they sorted, filtered or moved anything on it, is theirs and stays as it is, the old order with it, until they press Reset layout and filters; a list a person never touched, and every new person, has the new order. Asked whether the kept layouts should be put into the new order once, the owner chose to leave them alone. The capacity lines still have their vendor before their places, which the sequence does not name. A script read the choosers again: of 23 lists no two differ any more in Location and Site, in Operator and Owner or in Tags and Record status. | The owner, on 2026-11-18: "web interface, column list, each page should show the column order preferably in the same sequence when like fields exist across pages, such that Site is listed below Location when Site and Location are availble columns to add on a page; I think Location is listed before Site on one page and Site before Location on a different page, something to check and make consistent unless there is good reason to re-order the default sequence of columns." A script read the column chooser of the 23 lists that have one: the item list, the items of a vendor and the end of life had Site, Location, Planned location, Region, then Operator, Owner; the device groups had Owner, Operator, then Location, Planned location, Site, Region; the capacity lines Region before Site; the catalog Record status before Tags, against fourteen lists. Shown the three sequences side by side, the owner chose the smallest outward: Location, Site, Region, Planned location, then Operator, Owner. | 0.9.8 |
| P-254 | **A report of discrepancies, after 1.0.0.** A list of the gaps in the inventory that should be filled, each with a link to where its fix is begun: a container with no host, a virtual context with no switch, and the like, of which what is to be looked at (P-249) is the beginning. It need not be worked out as it is read: it is made when a person asks for it, with a measure of how far it has come or a notice when it is ready, and a setting turns on the checks once a day, which make the report by themselves. | The owner, on 2026-11-18, for the roadmap: "a discrepencies report that provides the list and a link to where a fix can be started for gaps that should be filled, such as a container with no host, a VDC with no switch, etc. Needn't be realtime, generate on-demand with a progress meter or notification when ready and a toggle to enable running the checks once per day that generate the report." | after 1.0.0 |
| P-255 | **Mail by a smart host, after 1.0.0.** `hirdir` sends mail by SMTP through a relay that the installation names, for notices and for reports, and above all for reports on a schedule: each day, the items that were newly discovered. | The owner, on 2026-11-18, for the roadmap: "SMTP routed via smart host relay for email notifications and reports, especially scheduled reports (e.g., daily report for new items discovered)." | after 1.0.0 |
| P-256 | **A report that is mailed carries its detail, a link, or both, after 1.0.0.** Where a report is sent by mail, a setting says whether the mail holds the report itself, a link to it as a page of `hirdir`, or both. The link asks for a sign-in unless said otherwise; an option lets anybody who has the link read that report. What such a link shows to somebody who is not signed in, and for how long, is to be settled when it is built: it is the one place where the inventory would be read without a sign-in. | The owner, on 2026-11-18, for the roadmap: "when reports are sent via email a toggle that sends the actual report detail via email and/or sends a link to the report to be viewed as a web page with option by default requires login to hirdir but an option to allow any with the link to view the report." The last sentence is the lead's note. | after 1.0.0 |
| P-257 | **A message of the day, after 1.0.0.** A message that an Admin writes is shown to everybody who signs in. | The owner, on 2026-11-18, for the roadmap: "Message of the Day (MOTD) that is displayed to all users that login." | after 1.0.0 |
| P-258 | **A maintenance mode, after 1.0.0.** An Admin turns it on: the application holds every import, export and other work that runs by the clock or by itself; shows Maintenance in progress; refuses every sign-in but an Admin's; and ends every session that is open, so that each person signs in again afterwards, keeping, where it can, the one session of the Admin who turned it on. | The owner, on 2026-11-18, for the roadmap: "Maintenance Mode where the application pauses any scheduled or automatic import/export or other timed processes, places a "Maintenance in Progress" message and refuses logins except from admins, and enforces all active sessions ended, users must re-login, preferably keep the one admin session active that enabled maintenance mode." | after 1.0.0 |
| P-259 | **Each connector is paused by itself, after 1.0.0.** Each call to an external system that is to come, and each other task that recurs, the hosts read from a monitoring tool, the hosts written to one, the sites read from another source of data, is paused and resumed by itself, and all of them at once. | The owner, on 2026-11-18, for the roadmap: "a pause-per-connector option where future calls to external systems and other recurring tasks can be set each to pause, or pause all, and each to resume, or resume all - such as import hosts from a monitoring tool, export hosts to a monitoring tool, import site locations from an external data source, etc." | after 1.0.0 |
| P-260 | **A field that must be filled says so at a glance.** The word `(required)` beside the label of such a field stands in the page's amber, on its ground and in bold, where it was small and grey; and the field itself has a mark down its left edge while it is still empty, which goes when it is filled. The words of the label are as they were, so that what reads a page aloud says what it said. It holds on every form, the mark being the one that every field of a form is drawn by. | The owner, on 2026-11-18: "For this round, if it fits, a highlight indicator on the required fields of each object / page, where (required) is used now on the required fields, consider a color for the word required, or other quick to identify method to assist an operator that is adding new items or other objects." It fitted: one rule of the style sheet. As built: an item, which needs one of several things to be known by and no one of them alone, has no field marked for it. | 0.9.8 |
| P-261 | **The browser scenarios leave the server a processor, and three costs of every request are taken away.** The server is one process and works on one processor. The browser scenarios, on the build host and in the package test, run one fewer file at once than the host has free, where they ran as many as it had free: a browser to every processor left the server none. And what its measurement found on the way is mended: the question whether anything runs on a host, which 0.9.8 asks before the first statement of every request, is asked of the two indexes of the host columns, where it read every item twice; the settings are read once for a transaction, where each was a statement of its own; and the server keeps as many connections to the database as it has threads, where connections were opened and thrown away under load. | The final checks of 0.9.8, alone on the build host of four processors, failed one to three browser scenarios in every run with four files at once, each waiting five seconds or more for a count, and passed all 225 with three at once in the same quarter of an hour; the owner, on 2026-11-19: "There is still budget available to find the bug, run the testing, then provide an update." Measured with a server that timed every request and sampled its own threads a hundred times a second, under the whole suite at four at once: the server had 0.93 of a processor from the first minute to the last; the 14,700 requests that only read, asked again one at a time, cost 285 seconds in all, and a request of 5 to 9 ms alone took 125 to 215 ms in the run; where three tables of the scoreboard stood still for five seconds, a summary of the first page, the coverage, the end of life, a sign-in and an import were in flight with them, and all ended together. Nothing waited on a lock, and no request was refused as busy. No one fault was found: the run asks more of the server than one processor gives, and 0.9.8 added to what each request costs, the walk through hosts some 60 ms to a summary and the question of hosts 5% of all the server did. With the three costs taken away the run at four at once was no different, the server being the measure of it either way; they are kept for what they save an installation, the first above all on a large inventory. What the walk through hosts costs stays with the timing of 1.0.0. | 0.9.8 |
| P-262 | **The server measures its own load, and past a ceiling answers at once to wait.** The server keeps, in its own memory, what it has done in the last five or ten minutes: how many requests it answered and how long each took, how many are in flight and how long the oldest has waited, and the processor time the process itself has used; an Admin reads it, on About or beside the settings, and nothing of it is stored. A setting is a ceiling: once the load is past it, a new request is answered at once, to wait and to try again shortly, with the time to wait, where it would have joined a queue and timed out; the page says so in words, as it does where the one writer is busy. What is shed first is what costs most and can wait, the reports and the long lists; a sign-in and a save are kept as long as they can be. The ceiling is later what says that an installation has outgrown one process: the trigger to put a second behind a load balancer, or to send people to an instance that takes the overflow, which wait for PostgreSQL, a second server on one SQLite file gaining little. The ceiling is two measures, and either one past its threshold brings the answer to wait: how long requests take to be answered, and the load of the processor where it is sustained. To be settled when it is built: the value of each from the start, and over how long a load counts as sustained. | The owner, on 2026-11-19, when the final checks of 0.9.8 had shown the server at the limit of one processor under four browsers at once (P-261): "keep notes for scaling and timing to revisit for 1.0.0. consider keeping metrics for the most recent 5 minutes or 10 minutes if the python process can track its own time and consider a safety cap that places a ceiling on performance, once the threshold is crossed new requests are given a quick "please wait and try again later" type response to avoid continuing to push the process beyond what it can handle under load without resulting in timeouts. this safety cap could be a future trigger to scale out behind a load-balancer or to redirect users to a spill-over second instance, maybe a topic to implement in 0.9.9?"; and, of the lead's note that scaling out waits for PostgreSQL: "agreed on postgresql before value can be obtained in horizontal scale-out." Asked what the ceiling is measured in, the lead recommending the requests in flight and the age of the oldest, the owner, the same day: "ceiling should be measured in both request response times and sustained CPU load, if a threshold of either is crossed then provide the back-off, try-again response"; and that 0.9.9 is these two things, P-262 and P-263, with a possible fix for the blank window that an operator's browser showed (P-230), of which the owner has more to tell. Measured for P-261: under load a request of 5 to 9 ms took 125 to 215 ms and the tables of the scoreboard stood counting for 20 to 30 seconds, every request in flight ending together, with nothing refused. On 2026-11-19, once the fault of the blank window was found (P-264), the owner put it out by itself: "Yes, release the fix as 0.9.9 and other actions move to 0.9.10." | 0.9.10 |
| P-263 | **The work that no person waits for has a process of its own.** What is not the answer to a person's request, the reports that are worked out at night and by the clock, the imports that are applied in the background, and later the exchanges with other systems, the hosts read from a monitoring tool and written to one, runs in a second process of `hirdir` on the same host, with the same database, so that it has a processor of its own and the answers to people are not slowed by it. The work is taken from the database, where an import that waits to be applied is a row already; the process that takes a piece of work is the one that owns it, in place of the rule that one server works a database; the package starts and stops the two together, and the package test holds both. Both write through the one writer of the database, so a long import still makes a save wait its turn: what is gained is the processor and the answers that only read. Each exchange with another system belongs in it from the start, where it is paused and resumed by itself (P-259). | The owner, on 2026-11-19, when the final checks of 0.9.8 had shown the server at the limit of one processor under four browsers at once (P-261): "also to consider, a dedicated process for internal data handling such as scheduled report generation, import and export tasks with external systems, and other work that isn't directly related to live end-user web requests. this will keep the python web request responsiveness in a different process, different core, than backroom work." In 0.9.8 the applies of imports and what runs by the clock are threads of the one server process; in the measured run, imports being applied were among what was in flight where the scoreboard stood still. On 2026-11-19, once the fault of the blank window was found (P-264), the owner put it out by itself: "Yes, release the fix as 0.9.9 and other actions move to 0.9.10." | 0.9.10 |
| P-264 | **Coming back to the tab of a list leaves the list as it was.** A list read again in the background, as the lists of choices are when the person comes back to the tab (P-183), is kept as the page has it where it holds the same values: nothing made from it is made again, so a table keeps its rows, its place and what is ticked. Where a value did change meanwhile, a table whose columns hold the lists is made again with them, and what follows a table, the source of its rows, asks nothing of the table just taken away and waits for the new one. | The owner, on 2026-11-19, of the window that 0.9.6 left blank and that 0.9.8 fills with the text of the fault (P-230): on Items, Circuits, Catalog, External systems and Capacity, "User clicks the page from menu bar, page renders. User clicks to different tab in the browser, then comes back to the page, error is generated", the text being `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'filter')`. The five are the lists whose columns are made from the lists of choices. Read again, the choices were other objects with the same values, so the columns were made again and the table with them; in the same pass the effect that gives a table its source of rows ran with the table already destroyed, which answers nothing when asked for the state of its columns. It happened on every return after five seconds, to every person. Reproduced on the build host with the server's own answers, the text and the place in the script being the owner's; two browser scenarios hold it, one for lists that are the same and one for a value edited meanwhile, each failing without its half of the change. The scenario of 0.9.7 that came back to a tab did so with answers of another shape, and so took this fault for one of its own making. | 0.9.9 |
| P-265 | **What the ceiling measures, and what it refuses.** The server keeps its last ten minutes in memory, in steps of five seconds: for each step how many requests it answered, how many well, how many it could not take as asked (`4xx`), how many failed (`5xx`), how many it answered as busy, the shortest, the average and the longest time an answer took, and the processor time the process used, as a percentage of one processor. Nothing of it is written anywhere, and it starts empty when the server starts. Two measures are read from it over the last `busy_over_seconds`, 30 unless set: **how long requests take**, the median of the time taken by the requests of the API answered in that time and of the time waited so far by those still in flight, where there are five or more; and **the processor**, the share of one processor the process used over it. Past `busy_response_ms`, 5000 unless set, or past `busy_cpu_percent`, 95 unless set, the server is busy; a measure set to 0 is not looked at, and both at 0 is no ceiling. While it is busy, what costs most and can wait is answered at once `503` Busy with `Retry-After`, in the words of a problem as the one writer's is: the reports, the exports, the previews of imports and the pages of lists. Still busy a further `busy_over_seconds` later, every read of the API is. Never refused: the page itself and its files, a sign-in, every change that is stored, the health routes, About and the server's own measures. An answer of busy is counted and is in neither measure. The three settings are the Admin's, under Settings, and hold from the moment they are saved. | P-262 left to be settled when it is built "the value of each from the start, and over how long a load counts as sustained". The values are the lead's until the owner gives others: under the load measured for P-261 a request of 5 to 9 ms took 125 to 215 ms and the tables that stood counting took 20 to 30 seconds, so five seconds as the median of half a minute is well past a slow moment and well before the minute at which a proxy gives up; the process, being one, cannot use more than one processor, and the whole browser suite at three at once holds it near 93% and passes. The median, not the average, so that one long export among quick answers is not a busy server; the requests in flight with it, so that a server from which nothing comes back is seen as busy and not as idle. Two steps, the reads that cost most first, are P-262's "what is shed first is what costs most and can wait". | 0.9.10 |
| P-266 | **Everyone signed in reads the server's measures, on About.** About shows, under what is running: whether the server is busy and by which measure; over the last ten minutes the lowest, the average and the highest time of an answer and share of the processor, and how many requests were answered well, not taken as asked, failed, and answered as busy; the requests in flight and how long the oldest has waited; the memory of the process, the memory of the host used and free, the space free where the inventory is kept, the processors of the host and its load; whether the process of the background work runs; and one graph of the ten minutes, the time of an answer and the share of the processor on one axis of time, with a mark at every step in which an answer of busy was given. It is one route, `GET /about/load`, which asks nothing of the database beyond what the sign-in asks, and is never refused as busy; the page asks for it every five seconds while About is on show in a tab that is in front, and not otherwise, and draws the graph itself, with no library. | The owner, on 2026-11-19: "add metric visibility on the about page, consider high/low/average numbers, counts for success, error, and busy page delivery, basic server values (memory used, memory free, disk free), and a graph that shows response times plotted alongside CPU usage, and identify on the graph when busy responses are provided. Keep low rendering the about page low as this will be watched by users as a troubleshooting tool and it shouldn't be a heavy spike in usage to display health and metrics. Consider tracking the past 5 to 10 minutes, and the metrics don't need to hit the disk unless there is real benefit to keeping the information over time; consider this an initial health monitoring capability that can be expanded after 1.0.0." P-262 had it read by an Admin; "watched by users" is later and is followed, the people signed in being the team's own. | 0.9.10 |
| P-267 | **The second process is `hirdir admin work`, a service of its own.** It applies the imports and bulk edits that wait, does what is done by the clock, the record of the week's capacity balance and the long reports worked out ahead, and sweeps the store of reference files when it starts; `hirdir admin serve` does none of it. It answers nothing on the network. It finds its work by looking in the database every second, where the server's own worker was woken by the route and looked every five; so an apply begins within a second of being asked for. One such process works a database: it holds `worker.lock` in the state directory as the server holds `server.lock`, a second one stops with words, and the commands that need the service stopped need both stopped. The package installs `hirdir-worker.service` beside `hirdir.service`, started, stopped and restarted with it, under the same limits. The server says whether the process runs, in `worker` of `GET /about/load`: `running`, `not_running`, or `in_process` where one process does both, which `hirdir admin serve --work` does for development and the tests of the routes do. A job that was applying when the process stopped is marked stopped when it starts, as it was when the server started. | P-263. The server and the second process share nothing but the database and the state directory: no signal passes between them, since a look each second costs one read of an index and a signal that went astray would leave a job waiting. The lock is asked for, not a file read, so nothing is written for it. | 0.9.10 |
| P-268 | **What the ceiling left open, as it was built.** The settings are read by an Admin while the server is busy, `GET /settings` being never refused, so that a ceiling set too low can be reached and put right. The check of answers that are kept and the preview of a bulk edit wait as the previews of imports do. The `503` of the one writer is counted as failed, not as busy: busy is the ceiling's answer alone, and its problem holds a member `busy`, with the step, the measure it is past and the seconds to wait, which the writer's has not. An answer of busy says to wait five seconds. Being busy begins when a request, or About, first finds the server so, nothing being looked at between requests. `GET /about/load` leaves the request that asks out of the requests in flight, and a value the host does not say is null. Four values are fixed and are listed with the settings for whoever reads them: five requests at the fewest for the measure of time, five seconds to wait, ten minutes kept, in steps of five seconds. The tests of the routes run with no ceiling unless a test asks for one, and so do the browser scenarios, but for the file that is of the ceiling. | The lane that built P-265, 2026-11-19, where the row was silent, each the safest reading: an Admin locked out of the setting that locks out would need the command line; a preview is work that can wait whatever it previews; and the writer's refusal is of one change, not of the server. Its note on the median stands in P-265 as written: with five requests in half a minute, three long exports alone are a busy server. | 0.9.10 |
| P-269 | **What the second process takes in 0.9.10, and what stays with the server.** The second process applies the imports and the bulk edits and records the week's capacity balance. Two things that P-267 gave it stay with the server for now. The long reports of the day, the coverage report, the renewal worklist, the summary of the renewal alert and the capacity balance, are worked out by the server, five minutes past midnight and at its start: what is worked out is kept in the server's own memory (D-184), so a copy worked out by another process would serve nobody. And the sweep of the store of reference files is the server's own, before its first request: the lock of the store is of the server's process, and a sweep from outside it could take away the part of an upload that is under way. An apply is stored `queued`, and the process that takes it up makes it `applying`: so one asked for while the second process is not running waits and is applied when it runs, and what is found `applying` when it starts is exactly what was cut short. | The lane that built P-267, 2026-11-19. The long reports are open with the owner, whose words for this release name "scheduled report generation" first: to move them, what is kept has to be kept where both processes read it, on disk or in the database, with what makes it stale, which is a piece of work of its own; and a kept answer is stale after any change, so by day it is worked out again by the server whoever worked it out at night. Before this, a job asked for was `applying` at once, which nothing told from one cut short. | 0.9.10 |
| P-270 | **The ceiling of time is the 95th percentile past three seconds, and every page shows the server's health.** In place of P-265's median past five seconds: the measure of time is the 95th percentile of the time of the requests of the last `busy_over_seconds`, with those in flight, taken with twenty or more of them, and `busy_response_ms` is 3000 unless set. What is long by nature, the reports, the exports and the previews, is not in it: one of them among nineteen quick answers would be the percentile; they are what is refused at the first step, and the pages of lists, which are in the measure, are refused with every other read at the second. The server's measures give the 50th, 95th and 99th percentile of the ten minutes and of each step, and count the requests by band of time: under a third of the ceiling, to two thirds, to the ceiling, and past it, which with three seconds is under one second, one to two, two to three, and over three. The health of the server is one of four words by the same bands, `good`, `slow`, `very_slow` and `busy`, and is `busy` whenever the server is, by either measure; every answer of the API carries it in a header, and the menu bar shows it as a mark, green, yellow, orange or red, with its word for whoever cannot tell the colours apart, which leads to About. The mark asks the server nothing: it is read from the answers the page gets anyway. The values stay the Admin's, under Settings. | The owner, on 2026-11-19, asked whether five seconds and 95% were right to start with: "Recommend making the threshold values available under admin settings, and longer than 3 seconds not 5 as a starting point, use p95 > 3 seconds as trigger, with counts for a few different levels of response times reported as metrics such as p50, p95, p99. Add a health bubble or icon on the menu bar, Green for p95 < 1s, Yellow for 1-2s, Orange for 2-3s, and Red for >3s, this allows users using the application to see health of the application with each page render. Feedback, alternative recommended?" The lead's readings, put to the owner: the bands as thirds of the ceiling, so that red is always the point at which the server refuses and one setting moves all four; twenty requests at the fewest, a percentile of five being its slowest; the long by nature left out and refused first, the lists refused second, since a trigger this early would otherwise refuse the most used pages while nineteen answers in twenty are quick; and the header in place of a request of the mark's own at every page. | 0.9.10 |
| P-271 | **What is refused follows the four colours.** In place of the two steps in time of P-265 and P-270: green and yellow refuse nothing; at orange, the 95th percentile past two thirds of the ceiling, what is long by nature is refused, the reports, the exports and the previews; at red, past the ceiling, every read of the API is answered as busy, the pages of lists among them. The page and its files, a sign-in, every change that is stored, the health routes, About with the measures and the settings are never refused. The processor past its own ceiling makes the server orange at the least, and never red by itself. After red the server is orange at the best for one further `busy_over_seconds`. The long reports of the day move to the second process in the release after 0.9.10 (P-269). The lists are not put into pages at yellow: a list already reads a hundred rows at a time as it is scrolled and never the whole, so pages with arrows would ask the server for no less; what a list costs is the count of everything that matches, at each read, which is a note for the timing of 1.0.0. | The owner, on 2026-11-19: "Correct, reports move in the release after 0.9.10. Restate how you recommend shedding load. I was thinking report the colors based on observed performance, and it's OK to exclude the intended-to-go-long import/export/large-reports, but the click quick-render pages are in scope, to include Items and Group lists which are likely to be the highest count number of objects to display, consider a threshold if over a certain limit make the default multiple pages with left-right arrows and go-to- number with a click to display all if the operator really needs the full list in one go, maybe trigger that reduced quantity when response times are yellow and above. Refuse nothing until orange at which point the heavy pages are refused, then at red the busy page is delivered." The lead's readings, put to the owner: the processor as orange and not red, answers being quick while it is the only measure past; and no pages at yellow, for the reason given. | 0.9.10 |
| P-272 | **The colour is seen with few requests; refusing needs load.** The measure of time is the 95th percentile of the requests of the last `busy_over_seconds`, thirty unless set, at any count: with twenty or fewer it is the slowest of them, so that one slow answer on a quiet server changes the colour, for as long as it is among those of that time; with none the server is green. What is refused follows the colour only where there is load to shed, twenty or more requests in that time: with fewer the mark says slow and nothing is refused for time, and About says that it is so. The mark's words are of speed where nothing is refused, and say busy only where something is; the header of every answer carries both, the health and what is refused. The processor past its ceiling makes orange only, and refuses the long whatever the count (the owner: "Agreed on both, CPU orange only and no paging at yellow"). | The owner, on 2026-11-19: "Should we use p50 instead of p95 for the threshold or is that too aggressive? I'm concerned on the point of 1 in 20 requests over what time period, 30 seconds? 5 minutes? If the average user experience which may be a low total page count, less than 20, starts to encounter slow pages it's no longer green."; and, of the lead's first answer, which took the third slowest where there are few so that one slow request would not change the colour: "Consider this threshold to catch poor performance in development more so than a popular website with thousands of users." So the one slow request is what the colour is for. The lead's answer on the rest: the median is the gentler of the two, red only when half of everything is past three seconds, and a page is several requests of which one slow one is a slow page. Thirty seconds, so that the colour and what is refused are of now and are over within half a minute; the ten minutes of About are the longer view. Nothing is refused on a few slow requests because the first page after a restart, or one slow page while an import holds the writer, would otherwise refuse the next press of the one person there. | 0.9.10 |
| P-273 | **About says what was slow, and for whom.** With the measures, About lists the ten slowest requests of the last ten minutes, each with when it came, its method and its route as a pattern, never the path or the words of a search, the time it took, its status, its request ID, by which it is found in the log, and who asked; the five slowest of what is long by nature are listed apart. And it lists the people who asked anything in those ten minutes, each with the number of requests, how many of them fell in each of the four bands of time, the slowest, how many were answered as busy and how many failed, and when the last came, whoever had it worst first; a service token by its name, and what says nothing of who asks, a sign-in among it, in one row together. Both are for everyone signed in, are kept in memory with the steps they belong to and leave with them, and are worked out when About asks, not for each request. | The owner, on 2026-11-19, of the lead's offer of the first list, a red mark saying that something was slow and not what: "Yes, add the slowest requests list to About in 0.9.10, and include a list of active users so the support team can see who is having a bad experience." The people signed in are the team's own, and their names are hidden from no role. | 0.9.10 |
| P-274 | **The server's measures, its health and what it refuses, as built.** This stands in place of the measure and the steps of P-265, by P-270 to P-273. The server keeps its last ten minutes in memory, in steps of five seconds: the requests answered well, not taken as asked (`4xx`), failed (`5xx`, or not sent to the last byte) and refused as busy; the shortest, average and longest time of an answer; and the processor time of the process, every thread, as a percentage of one processor, which passes 100 where several requests are in the database at once. Nothing is written, and it starts empty. A request's time runs from when the server has the whole of it, its body with it, to the last byte of its answer; until it is whole it is in flight and in no measure. Each route is long by nature (reports, exports, previews, the check of kept answers: in no measure), a read (every other `GET` and the `/query` routes), kept (sign-in, every stored change) or apart (health, About, settings, contract: in no measure). Over the last `busy_over_seconds` (30), how long requests take is the 95th percentile of the reads and kept requests that came in that time, with the wait of those in flight: the k-th slowest of n, k being n over twenty rounded up, at least one; null with none. The same rule gives the 50th, 95th and 99th of the ten minutes and of each step. The health is `good` under a third of `busy_response_ms` (3000) or with no request, `slow` to two thirds, `very_slow` to the whole, `busy` past it; and `very_slow` at the least where the processor is past `busy_cpu_percent` (95; 0 to 100 for each processor). Refused, with `503` Busy and `Retry-After`, by how long the reads alone take: the same percentile of the reads of the same time, a sign-in and a stored change left out. With twenty-one reads or more (`busy_fewest_requests`, so that the percentile is never one request alone), what is long where the reads are at two thirds of the ceiling or past it, and every read where they are past it; with fewer, nothing for time. Slow changes with quick reads make the mark red and refuse nothing; the health is never better than what is refused says. The processor refuses what is long at any count, never a read. A server that refused its reads refuses what is long for `busy_over_seconds` after; nothing else is remembered. Never refused: the page, sign-in, stored changes, health, About, settings, contract. A ceiling of 0 refuses nothing; the health is then said by the thirds of 3000. Every answer of the API carries `X-Hirdir-Health: <health>; refusing=<nothing, long or reads>`, as it is when the answer begins, the request's own time counted, so that the answer that was slow says so; a refused request carries what it was refused by. `GET /about/load` gives the health, the measure it stands on and what is refused; the percentiles and the counts by band; the ten slowest requests of the measure and the five slowest long ones, each by its route's pattern, with time, status, request ID and who asked; and who asked in the ten minutes with what each met, worst first: a service token under its account as such, what cannot be verified in one row, and past fifty accounts the rest together. A request refused before the sign-in is looked at is counted for its person where its session token verifies and the account is among the thousand last seen, at no cost to the database. The answer is some ten kilobytes at the most. In the page: the graph is two plots one above the other on one axis of time, each with its own scale; a read that a busy server refused has Try again within its words, and one press asks again for every such read on the page; and a run of the browser scenarios leaves its installation with no ceiling. | The lanes that built it, 2026-11-19, after an independent review of the first form found that a stall that was over kept the server busy and carried it further on an idle server, that the processor's share passes 100 since SQLite lets go of the interpreter while it runs a query, that five requests whose body never came held the server busy for good, that no test held the ways a request can end, and that two saves of the settings at one instant could leave the older ceiling in force: each mended and held by a test. A second review, of the rule as the owner had put it by then, found that changes waiting behind the one writer turned every read away and, the reads once refused, held the server red; that at twenty requests exactly one slow answer refused every read; and that the answer that was slow carried the health of the moment it came: so the reads alone decide what is refused, twenty-one of them at the fewest, and the header is of the moment the answer begins. The processor's "cannot use more than one processor" of P-265, and its median, are struck by this. | 0.9.10 |
| P-275 | **The second process works out the long reports of the day, and keeps them in the database.** In place of what P-269 left with the server, and of "in the memory of the server" and "when the server starts" in D-184, D-211 and D-224 for the four views that are worked out ahead: the coverage report, the renewal worklist, the summary of the renewal alert and the capacity balance, each for the view its page opens with, are worked out by the second process, when it starts and at five minutes past midnight, and kept in a table of the database, `kept_answers`, a row to each report: the request whole, the newest audit event and the day it is good for (D-184), the version of `hirdir` that worked it out, when it was worked out, and the answer. The server works out nothing ahead, at its start or by the clock. Asked for a report, it looks in its own memory, as before, and then at the row: where the row is of the same request, the same newest event, the same day and the server's own version it gives the row's answer and keeps it in its memory; where not, it works the answer out itself and keeps it in its memory alone, as it does every other view, which is kept nowhere else. The table is written by whoever works ahead, the second process, or a server that does both, and by nothing else but the check: a row is written only where the newest event is still the one its answer was worked out with, looked at again once the writer's place is taken, so that an answer worked out across a change is thrown away and not kept. What is kept is still no record: a row is never given once a change, another day or another version could have made it wrong, it is worked out again from the records, and nothing inherited is copied down. It is in the backup with the inventory it was worked out of and comes back with it, and a reset clears it. Where the second process does not run, nothing is worked out ahead and the first person to ask waits for the server, as after any change; About says that it does not run. `GET /kept-answers` lists, with what the server has given, what the table holds and would be given now, each with `ahead`, whether it was worked out ahead and is of the table; the check works those out anew too, and writes again a row that differed. Migration `0015` adds the table and changes no record. | The owner, on 2026-11-19: "Correct, reports move in the release after 0.9.10"; and on 2026-11-20 ("start 0.9.11"), asked where the second process should leave what it has worked out, with the case of a backup taken on a Monday, the reports worked out again on the Tuesday and the backup restored on the Wednesday, chose "In the database" over files beside it. In the database a kept answer goes where its inventory goes, into a backup, back from one and away at a reset, so that it cannot be given for another; and it is there for a second server once the database is PostgreSQL. A row to each report, not to each request: the table never holds more than four rows and nothing has to be swept, and the other views stay with the server, which alone is asked for them, so that no request that only reads has to wait for the one writer. The version is in the row since an upgrade may change how a report is worked out and writes no audit event. | 0.9.11 |
| P-276 | **By day the second process works the long reports out again once nothing has changed for half a minute.** Whenever it has no import or bulk edit to apply, each time it looks for work, the second process looks whether the four rows of P-275 are good for the present moment. Where one is not, and the newest change of the inventory or its settings is `reports_ahead_after_seconds` old or older, 30 unless set, it works out those that are missing and keeps them, on its own processor. So the reports are ready half a minute after the last of a run of changes, and within a second or two of midnight, when the day alone has made them stale. Somebody who asks sooner than that is answered by the server, which works the report out itself as it always has; the second process may then work out the same once more, since it cannot see into the memory of the server. While an import or a bulk edit is applied nothing is worked out ahead, each batch being a change, and one thread doing both. Set to 0, nothing is worked out again by day, and the reports are worked out when the second process starts and at five past midnight alone, which it does whatever the setting says. Working ahead that fails is logged and tried again a minute later, not at every look. The setting is an Admin's, a whole number of seconds from 0 to 3600, and holds from the next look. | The owner, on 2026-11-20, of the case that somebody retires a switch at a quarter past two, which leaves the reports of midnight stale, so that the next person to open the first page waits about four seconds on the fixture of 50,000 items while the server works them out on the processor that answers everybody: chose "After a quiet pause", once nothing has changed for 30 seconds, a value an Admin changes and 0 turns off, over "Night and start only", which is what the server did, and over "Right after every change", which works the reports out twenty times for twenty saves in a row and sets both processes to the same work where a person looks straight after saving. Without it the move gains the minute after midnight and the start alone, P-269 having noted that by day a kept answer is stale after any change. The age of the newest change is read from the audit event itself, so the second process remembers nothing between two looks. | 0.9.11 |
| P-277 | **What the review of the server of 0.9.11 found, and how it is put right.** *The newest event is the one stored last.* What a kept answer is good for (D-184) is the newest audit event of a change, and the newest was the one with the greatest HID. An HID is made from the clock of the process that makes it, so a change stored later had a lesser HID where the clock was set back, or where the server and the second process made theirs in one millisecond, and left every kept answer in use though the records had changed. The newest is now the event stored last: SQLite numbers the rows of a table as they are stored, under the one writer, and the audit trail is read back from its end by that number. It is in `db/dialect.py`; PostgreSQL has no such number, and the audit trail is to have a sequence of its own when PostgreSQL comes, until which the order there would be the HID's. A change dated after the present moment, as one is where the clock was set back since, counts as old enough for the reports to be worked out again (P-276). *The check writes as working ahead writes.* A check that found a row to differ wrote it at once, and so held the one writer's place while it worked out every report after it, for seconds on a large inventory, in which every save was refused; and it wrote the row with the stamp it had read at its start, over a good row where the second process had written one meanwhile. It now writes what differed when everything is compared, with the writer's place taken for those writes alone and the stamp read again, and only where the stamp is the same. *A check that a change overtakes stops.* The stamp is read again after each answer worked out anew: from the first for which it has moved, nothing more is compared, named or counted, since no kept answer would be given any more, and an answer worked out across a change is no defect of what was kept. Until now such an answer was named as one that differed, in 0.9.10 as well. `checked` is how many were compared. *One report that fails does not keep the others out.* Working ahead keeps each report that could be worked out, names in the log each that could not, and raises the first failure, which is tried again a minute later for that report alone. *The first page asks for the view that is kept.* It asked for the coverage of 90 days whatever `renewal_warning_days` said, so that with the setting changed the first page was never given the row, which is of the setting's days: it now leaves the days out, as the page of the report does, is given the kept view (D-184: "so that the home page and the report share one answer"), and says the days the answer is of. *`uses`* is as D-224 had it, how many times an answer has been given since it was worked out. And, from the second review, of these fixes: *the rows a check writes again and its event are one transaction*, stored together when the request ends, so that a row is never put right by a check of which the trail says nothing, as it was where the writer's place could not be had between the two. *The check says where a change overtook it*: `overtaken` in its answer and in its event, and the page says to check again when the inventory is quiet, where the Admin saw only a count smaller than the list. *When a service token was last used is stored at once*, in a transaction of its own as the request is recognised, and is not kept where the writer's place cannot be had in time: left to be stored with the request, as it was in every release that has tokens, it took the writer's place at the request's first read and held it until the request ended, so that a report or an export asked for with a token held up every save for as long as it took. *The hint under the days of the coverage report and of the end-of-life report* says the setting in force, where it said 90 and 730 whatever the settings were. And of the order of storing: SQLite's own documentation lets a VACUUM renumber the rows of a table such as the audit trail; measured on the version in use, a reset, a VACUUM, a `VACUUM INTO` and the online backup each kept the order, and a migration that ever makes `audit_events` anew is to copy its rows in that order. | Two independent reviews. The first, of commit d2e45e5, 2026-11-20, by a reader who had not built it, each finding shown by a test or a measurement: a save refused with `503` after five seconds while a check that had one row to write again took 12.8 s; a check overtaken by a change naming the capacity balance as a defect and writing its row under the event that was the newest no more; an event made two seconds before the newest by its HID leaving the server giving a supply of 3 where the records said 5, with nothing due; of 8,000 changes stored by two processes at once, 51 with an event older by HID than the newest; no row kept in two tries while the coverage report failed; and the first page's request worked out anew with the warning period at 60 days. The review found no way for working ahead, or for the server's reading of a row, to give a wrong answer otherwise: reads on SQLite are of each statement, so the stamp read before a report and again under the writer's place is what makes a row right. The owner had said, the same day, "Proceed until finished, test, publish", so each is the lead's reading, the safest: none changes what a person is shown, but for the first page's days where the setting was changed. The second, of the fixes at d84c644 by the same reader, found each new test failing with its fix taken out but one, which holds the server's side of the first page; a save that waited 1.56 s behind a coverage report of 2.5 s asked for with a service token, and 2.1 s behind a check so asked for, the fix of the check notwithstanding; and a check answered `503` after its row was written, whose second asking found nothing to name. Left as it is, and noted for the timing of 1.0.0: the read of the audit trail at each look of the second process, and at each request for a kept report, goes back past every sign-in, preview and download since the newest change: 32 ms past 200,000 of them, measured by the lead with the order of storing, where the review had measured 84 ms with the order of the HIDs. | 0.9.11 |
| P-278 | **While the form of a row is open, the row's buttons stand at the foot of the row, beside the form.** D-238 has the form of a row open directly beneath it, with the row in view above the form. A row taller than the window has left for it cannot be in view whole with the form's first field, and the button that opened the form, at the head of the row, was what went out of sight. So while its form is open, the cell of a row's buttons is set at the foot of the row: Change, with the mark that it is open, is directly above the form, and whoever looks at the form sees what opened it and what closes it. When the form closes the buttons are at the head of the row again, where the focus goes back to Change and the window with it. It holds wherever a form opens beneath a row, Admin › Settings, Tags, Lifecycle rules and Files, and the Files and links of a record; in a row of one line nothing moves. The descriptions of the settings are left as long as they need to be. | The owner, on 2026-11-20, after 0.9.11 was published, asked what the Settings page should do with a tall row, keep the Change button beside the form or shorten the long descriptions: "Keep the Change button beside the form". Measured on a window of 1280 by 800 before the change: the rows of `busy_response_ms`, `busy_cpu_percent` and `reference_file_types` are 733, 444 and 526 pixels tall, and with the form open and its first field 380 pixels from the top of the window, their Change buttons were 558, 227 and 490 pixels above the top of the window. The scenario of D-238 tried the first setting and the last, and so met the fault only when a setting with a long description became the last, in 0.9.11, whose description was shortened for it; it now tries the tallest row as well. That the buttons move only while the form is open, and not for good, is the lead's reading: closed, a row reads from its name to its buttons along its first line, as every table does. | 0.9.12 |
| P-279 | **The second process writes no line when it has worked the long reports out.** It writes one where working ahead failed, and one for each report that could not be worked out (P-276, P-277); when it succeeds, nothing. `GET /kept-answers` says when each report was worked out. | The owner, on 2026-11-20, asked whether a line was wanted in `journalctl -u hirdir-worker` each time the reports are worked out, a couple of lines a minute on a busy day: "no log line". It is as 0.9.11 was built; this records the answer. | 0.9.11 |
| P-280 | **Who is a user, a contact and an operator is said on each of the three lists, as a link, and each list finds the gaps.** A user is the contact it names, and an operator is the contact behind it (P-172); one contact may stand behind several operators. `GET /users` and `GET /users/{hid}` gain `operators`, the operators whose contact is the user's, each with `hid`, `label`, `colour` and `record_status`, none for a user with no contact. `GET /contacts` and `GET /contacts/{hid}` gain `operators`, the same of the contact, beside `is_user` and `user`, which they have. `GET /operators` has `user` and `scope` already. The Users list shows the contact and the operators of each user as links, the Contacts list the user and the operators of each contact, the Operators list the user behind each operator: a mark that is a link to the other record, and plain words where there is none. Each list is narrowed to its gaps: users with no contact and users with no operator (`GET /users` takes `contact` and `operator`, each `any`, `some` or `none`), contacts that are no user and contacts behind no operator (the filter fields `is_user`, which there is, and `is_operator`), operators with no user behind them (`scope`, which there is). A service account is no person and is in no gap. A user that is disabled is in a gap as any other is: the account is still there to be put right or removed. A contact that is retired is as no contact: a user whose contact is retired is among those with no contact and, nobody being behind its operators, among those with no operator, and still answers both, so that what is left over of a person who left is found; until the three are retired together (P-291). | The owner, on 2026-11-20, of the round after 0.9.12: "goal is to improve the daily operator and admin tasks in hirdir, reviewing data, importing, exporting. hirdir must be easy to use, fewer clicks, to encourage teams to maintain their data in the system. data that is easy to update gets updated." The owner's list: "Highlight users that are contacts and vice versa" and "Highlight users that are operators and vice versa"; and, of the lead's recommendation that the highlight be a mark that is a link, with a filter for the gaps, which are what an Admin acts on: "Agreed with recommendations listed". The people signed in are the team's own, and their names are hidden from no role. That a disabled user stays in the gaps was the reading of the lane that built it, told to the owner, who answered on 2026-11-20: "Agreed on the two readings the lane made." Two things the lanes chose on the pages, told to the owner when it was published: the Users list at 1280 pixels puts the email beneath the name and both numbers in one cell, so that the notes are in view with no scrolling; and the Operators list offers two gaps, No user behind it and Nobody behind it, an operator with a contact that is no user being other than one with no contact. The owner, on 2026-11-20, after it was published: "Agreed on all five calls." | 0.9.13 |
| P-281 | **A user has notes.** `users.notes`, free text of the length a contact's notes take, blank unless given: what the account is for, who asked for it, when it is to end. It is the account's, beside the notes of the contact, which are the person's; a service account, which is no person, has these alone. `POST /users` and `PATCH /users/{hid}` take `notes`, `GET /users` and `GET /users/{hid}` give it, the Users list shows it and the user's page changes it. A change of it is audited as any change of a user is. They are an Admin's to read as to write: what a person's own sign-in answers, `GET /auth/me` and the answer of signing in, never holds them. Migration `0016` adds the column and changes no record. | The owner, on 2026-11-20, of the round after 0.9.12: "goal is to improve the daily operator and admin tasks in hirdir, reviewing data, importing, exporting. hirdir must be easy to use, fewer clicks, to encourage teams to maintain their data in the system. data that is easy to update gets updated." The owner's list: "Add a notes/comment field for users". The review of the server found that a person read the notes on their own account; asked on 2026-11-20, with the case of an account noted as on trial, whether a person should, the owner chose "No, Admins only". | 0.9.13 |
| P-282 | **A user's groups are changed from the user's page, and given when the user is made.** The page of a user lists the groups it is in, each with the role the group grants, with a box to each group there is: ticking and unticking add and remove the user at once, by the routes there are, `PUT` and `DELETE /user-groups/{hid}/members/{user_hid}`, each audited as now. `POST /users` takes `group_hids`, the groups the new user is put into in the same change, each audited; a group that is not there refuses the whole. The owner's list: "Add/remove to groups from user detail page, and when creating new users". Until now a membership was changed from the page of the group alone. | 0.9.13 |
| P-283 | **A new user is made with its contact and its operator in one step.** The New user form has two boxes, both ticked from the start for a person: Also a contact, with the name, the email and the numbers, and Also an operator, with the label, which is the username until it is typed over, and the colour, which is the next that no operator has (P-285); with the role and the groups (P-282) on the same form, one Create makes the user, the contact and the operator, and shows the access code. Unticked, the operator is not made, as for somebody who works no equipment. A service account has neither. `POST /users` takes `operator`, an object with `label`, `colour` and `description`, each optional: left out, the label is the username and the colour the next unused. A colour sent blank or as `null` is no colour, as it is for an operator made by itself: only a colour left out is chosen by the server. It needs a contact, new or there, in the same request, and is refused with a `422` on `operator` without one. All of it is one transaction, each record audited as if it were made by itself; where any part is refused nothing is made. | The owner, on 2026-11-20, of the round after 0.9.12: "goal is to improve the daily operator and admin tasks in hirdir, reviewing data, importing, exporting. hirdir must be easy to use, fewer clicks, to encourage teams to maintain their data in the system. data that is easy to update gets updated." The owner's list: "Simplify onboarding when an admin creates a user, checkbox or button to make it clear, simple, fewer clicks to create linked contact and operator records when a new user is created"; and, asked with the case of a new engineer whether the operator's box starts ticked, unticked or follows the role: "Contact and operator ticked". Until now the operator was a second visit, to another page. That a blank colour is no colour, and only one left out the next unused, was the reading of the lane that built it, told to the owner, who answered on 2026-11-20: "Agreed on the two readings the lane made." That the New user form sends the colour it shows, so that what is seen is what is made though another operator took that colour meanwhile, was the lane's choice, told to the owner when it was published, who answered on 2026-11-20: "Agreed on all five calls." | 0.9.13 |
| P-284 | **An operator is made of a user in one press, and the operator's page reads details first.** The page of a user who is behind no operator, and the page of a contact who is behind none, has Make an operator: one press makes an operator whose contact is that person, with the username as its label, or the contact's name where the contact is no user, and the next unused colour (P-285), and leads to it. In the New operator form, choosing a person who is a user fills the label with the username while the label is still blank or still what the form filled in.
